DRUDENTIAL ASSURANCE COMPAN LIMITED CHM OFFICE 3 HoLßoriN BAW3, LONDON. Summary of the R-port presented at the Fifty-ninth Annual Meeting, held on sth •March, 1903. ORDINARY BRANOII.—The numbar of policies Issue 3 during the year was S 6 060, assuring the suns of £8,156,845, and rroducing a new annual premium Income of £468,357 The premiums received during the year were £1,481,377, being an increase of £189,106 over the year 196. The claims of the year amounted to £2430117. The number of deaths was 8,243, and 12,328 imdowment assurances matured. The number of policies in force at the end of the year was. 843,206. INDUSTRIAL BEA.NCIi.—The premiums received during the year were £6,661,671, being an ineresse of £162,603. The claims of the year amounte i to £2,563,090. Tne numoar of deaths was 281,990,and 1,494 endowment a asuranoes matured The number of free policies granted during the year to those policyholders of five years' standing and upwards who desire to discontinue their payments, was 128.429, the slumber ha force being 1,290,758. The number of free polices which became claims during the year was 32,104. The total number if policies in to ce at the end of the year was 17,45°,835; their average duration exceeds eleven years. The assets of the company, in both branches, as shown in the balance sheet, are £68,006,?.34. being an increase of £4,119,276 over those of 1908. In pursuance of the policy indicated in the last report, a scheme was approved by the Shareholders at meetings held in July last, admitting Industrie' Branch policyholders, and the Superintendents, Assistant Superintendents, and Agents to specified shares in the profits of the Company. The Directors are pleased to announce that In the Ordinary Branch a reversionary bonus at the rate of £1 12e. per cent. on the original sums assured has again been added to all classes of participating policies issued since the year 1876. In the Industrial Branch a bonus by way of addition of 5 per dent. to the sums assured will be paid on all policies of over five years' duration. which become claims either by death or maturity of endowment from the 6th of March, 1908, to the 4th of March, 1909, bath dates inclusive. WARRINGTON CRICKET CLUB.

Annual Meeting.

The annual meeting of the Warrington Cricket and Bowling Club was held at the Lion Hotel on Monday evening under the presidency of Alderman Forshaw. There was a good attendance. which included Aldermen Tinnion and Pierpoint. Mr. R. T. Fairclough, Mr. C. H. tinsworth (hon. secretary), and Mr. R. H. Silcock (hon. treasurer).

THE FINANCIAL POSITION. Mr. R. H. Sncocx presented the balance sheet, which showed that the balance due to the bank had during the year increased from £l2O 3s. 2d. to £153 Os. 4d. He explained that the large deficit was accounted for by the expenses incurred in repairs to the fence and pavilion which amounted to £6l its. ed. A capital and inexpensive idea is to trace upon thick brown paper : animals. figures &c., cut them out carefully ; and finish them off with dashes of Indian ink, and white paint. They should then be pasted on the walls, care being taken to render the effect harmonious, and artistic. THE TINY HANDEERCHIEF.

We have taken to those that slip into our gloves for evening wear, says the Queen, perhaps because we have no pocket in which to bestow any others, and it is somewhat old fashioned nowadays to carry a handsome lace-edged hand- kerchief in the hand. Glove handkerchiefs, how- ever, Are bordered with lace, with the monogram worked in the corner. A coloured border, with the monogram worked in the same hue, is erten considered a fitting accompaniment to a tailor- made gown, and the purse handkerchief is just a little larger than the glove handkerchief, but has no lace at the edge. It depends for its ornamen- tation on the monogram, and should be of the finest possible cambric. In the summer, when we are wearing linen gowns, embroidered with some particular flower, the tiny handkerchief bordering of the same, and many peopl aye their names on their handkerchiefs embro ered in an exact facsimile of theit- own handwriting.

TO-DAY'S RECIPE. EGG Jr:lieu:S.—Break four fresh eggs into a basin and whisk them lightly, then add an ounce of finely chopped cooked ham or tongue and a good pinch of mixed savoury herbs, also a tea- sooonful of finely chopped parsley; then beat up the mixture and season with very little salt and pepper. Have ready three or tour slices of toasted bread, cut them to size and butter them. Melt three-quarters of an ounce of butter in a saucepan, put in the egg mixture and stir it over the fire till just setting; then spread it on tiae prepared toast and serve hot,,

WOMEN'S CHAT.

McDOUGALL'S Pewit SELF-RAISING FLOUR TaY7ll2 D.

thick silk, upon, preferably, plain materials. Some WOMAN'S REALM. girls who possess names of say four or five letters, embroider one letter upon each button, placing the buttons so that the name reads from top to bottom. The idea is novel, but savours slightly of the name brooch, which is happily rarely seen nowadays. "Allen Rains."—This popular Welsh novelist has THE LATEST IN HATS. happily recovered from her recent serious illness,and is Of all articles of the toilet the hat is the one now at work upon a new novel. The story finished before we like to vary and renew most often, and our her illness, entitled " Neither storehouse nor Bern ' will be published by Messrs. Hutchinson this week, a modistes foster this whim of ours, says our Paris fact not likely to be overlooked by Allen Elaine's count- correspondent, by making hats that render us less admirers, who have long been looking forward just as pretty as we wish, and prettier than we eagerly to another work from her pen. are. Among the many characteristics that ap- MADGE. pertain to the millinery models of the moment, I notice a tendency to elongate the brim of the hat at the back, leaving it quite narrow at the sides and front. This is especially effective with the new mode of hair-arrangement, that is to say, much breadth across the crown, no hair at all upon the forehead, and more bouffant at the back than has hitherto been liked. A few huge hats are still seen, but oddly trimmed in a flat surface that slopes from the centre of the crown to the brim, shirred ribbons making this surface, a couple of bands of fur, with a series of widely. separated roses pinned on between, helping to completely conceal the shape of the hat. This absurd thins-, set low on the head, is rendered most becoming (and it somehow does manage to be very becoming to the Pari,ienne who dons it) by a veil or simulated veil in plain gauze bound with a tiny roll of velvet which stands out stiffly all around the edge like a scant ruffle. Some- times the ruffle is fuller, and is of lace tucked in- side near the hair, white, of course, and im- mensely attractive against well-trained, well- kept locks. __

SUGAR AND SPICE.

The old nursery rhyme tells that these are the two things which little girls are made of. Now, although girls are not lnlde of sugar, yet if they did not eat sugar in some form they would presently cease to be girls. Currants are over two-thirds their weight natural ugar, ready for digestion, and are therefore one of Nature's best and cheapest foods. Girls and boys should eat Currants every day to be strong and healthy.

How Strength and Rosy Cheeks were Restored by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills.

" She was like a ghost ! " was the appropriate description given by Mrs. Annie Horrocks of her daughter Martha; a remark that is commonly heard of the many fragile victims of acute Anmia. Mrs. ' Annie Horrocks, when interviewed at 6, Howards Yard, Clayton Street, Wigan, continued : - -

THE TRIAL OF TIIE TEENS. "Five years ago, soon after Martha entered her teens she became quite an invalid through fainting fits. Common medicine failed to give her any relief and within a few months from her first attack, she wasted away to skin and bone. " She could not bear to be on her feet for long because of terrible backaches • and month after month she was racked with fearful headaches. Her cheeks were livid and pinched as though she were starved, and her eyes were dark and lifeless looking. Her lips and gums showed how bloodless she was for they were a waxy white.

===== 0002601_19080321_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
SINKING RAPIDLY.

" We did all we could for Martha, but she sank rapidly day by day, and we could not tempt her to take any solid nourishment because her stomach !evoked against food of evey kind. For months she existed on practically nothing but broths ; occasion- ally she would take a little bread and eak tea, but only morsels for fear of sickness.

4 WELL CLOTHED YET EVER COLD. "In spite of warm clothing Martha would sit by a blazing fire, shivering all the time, and saying her blood felt frczen. She was advised to take fresh air and exercise, but the %least exertion left her puffed and panting and everyone c( uld see she suffered from a palpitating heart. Then night after night she complained of neuralgia. "It happened then that an important cure by Dr Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People came to my knowledge so I determined to give my. daughter a course of the Pihs.

(Applause). Alderman EVANS then moved that a similar com- pliment be paid to Mr. H. Roberts for his services as deouty-chairman. Mr. JOLLEY in seconding said that whilst Mr. Roberts and himself had on many occasions been in antagonism to one another they had always been personally very good friends and he thought there was a real sympathy between them, because they both seemed to have the knack of frequently getting into hot water. (Laughter.) They had both an equally keen desire to seeedncational work carried on on the best possible lines and in the interests of the community as a whole. Mr. PLINSTON supported. He said that when he had the courage to propose Mr. Henry Roberts as vice-chairman there were certain people who thought for certain reasons that he was not a fit person to occupy the position and that perhaps the abilities of Mr. Roberts might be used detrimentally to the interests of some members of the committee. He was pleased to know that Mr. Roberts had the ability and the courage of his convictions and was able,if called upon, t o take charge of that authority in an able manner. He hoped that if it should happen that the chairman some day declined to continue the duties—not because he was sent to the House of Lords —(laughter)—that Mr. Roberts would be elected to succeed him and he was convinced that Mr. Roberts would show he was a very able man to look after the interests of education in Warrington. The CHAIRMAN also supported and said Mr. Roberts was an educationist of the first water.

The vote was cordially passed. Mr. ROBERTS returned thanks. He said he would have liked Mr. Jolley to have left the reference as to their similarity in certain directions alone—(laughter) —because he had been flattering himself of late years that he was improving a little. (Laughter.) He would like also to be able to say the same of his friend. (Laughter.) Possibly some day before very long.he might be able to say it with absolute accuracy. (Laughter.) It was perfectly true that he (Mr. Roberts) did feel keenly everything which concerned the great question of education. To his mind there was no other question which was fraught with anch importance and such great possibilities both for the future of the town and of the nation. Whatever ,phase of it came forward, leaving the controversial religious element out of consideration altogether, whether it concerned secondary, technical, or elementary education, in all those matters he felt a keen and, he hoped, a zealous interest. He was anxious that the town of his birth should take its place, as it was rapidly doing, amongst the first towns of the country in regard to every branch of education in order that the children of Warrington might stand well with all the children of the Empire. As far as in him lay, he would do everything he possibly could, without fear or favour and without having regard to any special personal considerations, to further the great interests which he believed every member of that authority had sincerely at heart. (Applause.)

A REMOSPECT.

'Tans moonlight, and the sky was clear, A cloudless sky serene : When musing o'er some memories dear I paced a rural scene. Adown my face some trickllng tears Unbidden slowly fell, As I recalled the distant years And scenes remembered well. At first, along a winding brook, The path through meadows led ; Then through the corn my way I took, With slower, gentler tread. Upon my right a straggling wood As in the days gone by ; In all its leafy glory stood Outlined against the sky. And there, in childhood's happy hours, It made my heart rejoice To gather wild, uncultured flowers, And chase the Cuckoo's voice. Or, when the storms of early spring Shook it with wildest blast ; 'Twits joy upon a stein to cling, And feel the bending mast. And on my left a lonely lake, Lay bathed in Luna's glow ; In whose wide fringe of ferny brake Some wild birds nestled low. In front were undulating hills With peaks of rugged mould ; And down their slopes some babbling rills Made music as they rolled. But sounds of life no longer stirred The landscape passing fair ; No voice of man, no voice of bird Relieved the silence there. 'lde hamlet all in slumber lay, The bleatings too were o'er, Till lovely morn, with quickening ray, Would life and joy restore. But oh, my friends were dead and gone, All laid in holy ground ; And Resurrection's call alone Will break their sleep profound And while f thought, with throbbing hear Of each belov'ed face ; Hew little seemed man's earthly part-, How small his resting place ! J. H. W. CLTNDINNING.

March, 1909.

The Examilier, Saturday, March 21, 1908.

NATURE NOTES. ---- March Wild Flowers.

With the advent of the month of March the army of the flowers may be said to have begun their yearly ; march through the meadows and the woodland groves j and glens, over the moors and the mountains, and : along the sea shores. Already several of the advanced ; scouts have appeared as if spying out the land. Fore- most and daintiest of them all, in spotless white, ; graceful and strong, the lovely little snowdrop has ' appeared on the scene a full fortnight in advance of its fellows. The snowdrop, though so delicate and _ fragile in appearance, affords one of the most striking ! - examples of the power of growth in plant life. On ! two occasions I have seen this flower making its way j ; into the world under the most adverse circumstances ; once it was forcing itself through an asphalted garden walk, and at another place a macadamieed country roadside, well-worn and hard, was not Sufficient to ' restrain its exuberant life. A contemporary of the snowdrop has already ; appeared in meadows and parks in certain localities, and like it still remains with us to cheer on the advancing host. This is the winter aconite, one of ! the buttercup order of plants, a low-growing plant with a bright yellow flower, and large leaf-like bracts ; circling round the base of the blossom giving a pretty study of harmony in gold and green. The plant is not very common round Warr6gton, but it is frequently I met with in gardens. Should the weather be mild and ; the east winds not too keen during the month many of ! the advanced guard will make their appearance. Most of them are already well on the way, and only await a few hours genial warmth to show their heads. The first to break into flower is almost sure to be the spring I purple crocus (crocus vernus) and its yellow relation of the garden (crocus luteus). Both these plants are better known in our public parks and private gardens than in the wild state, yet in some parts of England ; they are very common indeed. The ovary of this flower is quite underground, hidden amongst the bases of the leaves out of the way of the frost. When the pollen grain reaches the stigma it sends out a pollen tube which will have to travel five or six inches down the style before fertilization can take plac?, a journey which r occupies from one to three days. After the formation of the seed the stalk below the ovary elongates and pushes the fruit out of the ground for the sun to ripen ; the seeds. That feathered wanton, the sparrow, plays havoc amongst the yellow crocuses in some districts ; by pulling the petals to pieces. What is the reason of this? There seems to be some purpose behind this vandalism. It seems hard to think that the bird acts out of pure spite. A curious ; fact is that both field mice and house mice show the same preference for the yellow crocus. These rodents will in a night visit a garden border, choose all the yellow flowers, passing over the blue ones, dig up the corms and devour them. In the morning a number of little pits will be found where the destructive little snavvies have been at work. The naughty sparrows may act under the same occult attraction of the yellow flowers, but are either not sufficiently industrious to finish their work or are prevented by frequent inter- ruptions from getting to the buried food store. It is very remarkable that it is invariably the yellow plants which suffer, because it has been found that the yellow crocus corm has a more pleasing taste than the purple one. So far as the human nose can find out there is no difference in smell between the two colours, but it is, of course, quite possible that the keener scent of the sparrow and the mouse may detect one.

Another favourite which will be emerging from its I long winter's sleep and wafting its fragrance on the balmy air is the sweet violet. It still escapes ..he t rapacity of the " penny-a-root " hs wker in some sheltered districts, and may be found on woody banks usually with a south aspect. As with many other violets there are two colours of flowers—blue and white. What is the real cause of this albinism in plant life no one has yet discovered. Many theories have been given, but none seem conclusive. But what is stranger still, is, that most of the violets besides having flowers of two colours, bear two kinds of flowers, one with the usual coloured petals and another later on in the season, entirely without petals. The petaloid flowers seldoln develop seed, white the petal- less flowers, which never open, produce seed in abund- ance.

The daffodil is, ear excellence a March flower. It has many names, it is the " Daffy," " Daffy-Down- Dilly " of village children in the Midlands, the "Glen" , of the West of Scotland, and in Cornwall it is known as the " Lent Lily." This is a flower of the woodland; and a lover of shade. The plant must not he con- fused with the narcissus of Greek poetry, which has a sweet-scented white flower. The tube-shaped corona in this flower is an "extra" not found in many British plants. Of the six outside floral leaves the three outer ones are sepals and the three inner ones petals. In the " double ' varieties, now so abundant in the florists' windows,all the stamens have been transferred by the art of the gardener into petals. Therefore a ' double flower can never produce seed, the essential organs having been traded for show. The coltsfoot and the butterbur are two plants of the composite order which may be found in flower this month, the former in rough waste ground, and the latter generally in abundance in damp places by banks of rivers and brooks. Neither plant has the graceful beauty of the snowdrop or the daff Ail, their beauty is that of proportion and mass rather than outline. The growth of the flower stem in both plants between the fading of the flowers and the ripening of the fruit is well worth observing. The elongation of the flower-stalk is a contrivance to lift the fruit above

..„ the surrounding vegetation (including their own immense leaves which at this stage develop very rapidly) so that the wind' may have full play upon the feathery pappus and transfer he se3l amAy t rona the parent plants. In the copses and shady hedgerows if there is warmth and wind the wood anemone will be sure to show the passer-by its white star-like flowers. The more the wind blows the wid Sr will they expand, a habit which has given the plant the name of vt ind- flower. The anemone belongs to the butt ircups, and it is an interesting object for examination. The under- ground stem, the single flower-stalk bearing leaf-like bracts, and the apparent absence of sepals tell some- thing of the relationship between habit and structure. Many spring flowers, usually white or some pale colour, have a tendency to become more or less red Amongst these we find red specimens of the anemone. This red tint is more prevalent in bleak springs, and in exposed situations. Red colour has the power of absorbing heat rays from the sun more than other colours, and this in cold springs helps to protect the young flower, inside the unopened bud, from the biting winds and cold. Toward the end of the month the snowy white flowers of the blackthorn will be claiming our atten- tion by their beauty. A large solitary bush in a hedge in full bloom is a very striking picture with the white flower mass set off against the black leafless branches. In the Craven district of the West Riding especially at Kettlewell and round Grassington where the hedges are almost entirely composed of blackthorn, the eight of the long lanes of white-topped hedges is one of the very finest in English landscape. . .

The ground it, a plant 'less ambitious than the blackthorn, may be found in the shelter of hedge bottoms with its scented kidney-shaped leaves putting forth its modest purple lip-shaped flowers. Among the trees, in thicket and hedge, the hazel erect or semi erect male catkins will be lengthening, opening out and becoming Pendulous, shedding their abundant pollen in the breeze over the tiny crimson star-like female flowers below. By river and stream the alder has opened its long dangling male catkins full of pollen, and the more slender upright female catkins will be swaying in the wind. On the same tree the dried, hard, brow. cones of the previous year may still be seen clinging to the twigs. What the fierce blasts of winter could not accomplish will soon be done by the rising new life within the tree, and one by me the dead cones will soon be left lying under the tree.

The flowers of the other catkin-hearing trees, the willows, the black and white poplars' and the aspen, will be fully developed before the end of the month. Then comes the burst of the varying tints of green leaf o'er woodland and meadow, bringing gladness and joy to weary eyes. Battalion after battalion and brigade after brigade of colour will be marching with quick step over the landscape till the middle of May when the spring flowers will be on toll parade. G. A. DUNLOP. The Municipal Museum, Warrington.

INFIRMARY EXTENSION.

OPENING BY LORD GROSS.

£15,000 Raised, But Another £l,ogo Required.

Warrington can now pride itself upon the possession of an Infirmary thoroughly adequate in accommoda- tion and equipment to meet the increasing needs uf the town, for Thursday witnessed the opening of the extension which of late years has become an absolute necessity. The new buildings consist of a pavilion and nurses' home and the everior of both is of the Georgian character, with plain-brick work and terra- cotta dressings. The pavilion is three storeys high and gives 66 additional beds to the institution,which is connected with the main central corridor of the present building on each floor. As, hovvever,it is only proposed to use two of the three new wards at present, the additional beds at once placed at the disposal of the institution will be 44. Each floor opens on to a covered verandah on the south side, for convalescent patients to enjoy the open air in suitable weather. The floors of the building throughout are of fireproof construction. The building will be heated with independent hot-water apparatus, supplemented by double-faced faience stoves with descending flues. Fire escape staircases are arranged from the first and second floors, diverging on to the ground at different points. The position of the Nurses' Home is south- ward of the new pavilion. It accommodates at the present time the matron, two sisters, twenty-four nurses, with further accommodation for the servants in connection with the home. It is arranged so that an additional twelve nurses can be accommodated, . _

should the Institute be again enlarged. Great credit is due to the architects(Hessrs. William Owen and Son) for designing such a suitable block of buildings and to the builder (Ur. C. W. Davenport) for the admirable way in which the work has been carried out.

It was very appropriate that the Right Hon. Viscount Cross, G.C.8., should perform the opening ceremony, , for his lordship opened the old Infirmary in 1877 and Ihe was at one time very closely identified with the tolvn. Fortunately the event was favoured with a pleasant spring-like day, and there was a very large and representative gathering The Town Hall, where his lordship was received by &le Mayor, looked quite bright with its decorations of flags and streamers, and the exterior of the Infirmary was similarly embellished.

THE PROCESSION.

A procession was formed headed by the Volunteer Band, discoursing lively selections under the direction of Bandmaster Richardson. The hon. medical officers, wearing their caps and gowns, followed, and next came members of the Board of Management. Lord Cross and other ladies and gentlemen rode in carriages. AT THE INFIRMARY.

On arrival at the Infirmary, Lord Cross and party,. proceeded through the building to the verandah on the ground floor of the new pavilion where the opening ceremony took place. The hymn" Thou to Whom the sick and dying" was sung to the accompaniment of the Volunteer Band and afterwards the Rev. W. Bracecamp offered prayer. Sir GILBERT GRIENALL as President of the institu- tion, then presented to Lord Cross a gold key and asked him to open the new premises. Lord Citoss, having accepted the key, said : Sir Gilbert Greenall,ladies and gentlemen. In the year 1377, more than thirty years ago,l had the honour to lay the foundation stone of increased buildings of this Infirmary. You have given me the pleasant duty again of doing something towards further buildings. I gladly accepted the invitation and am here to-day in consequence. On looking at the paper which has been placed in my hands, I must congratulate the town and yourselves upon two matters. In the Bret place, I can readily conceive from the statistics here given, how the town and neighbourhood have increased in prosperity, how the works have grown and how the population has grown also. As the population has grown, of course the needs of the. Infirmary had grown and although since 1877 one addition—a very considerable addition— was made many years ago, it has now been found necessary that there should be further accommodation provided and we are here for that purpose at the present moment. Therefore, if I congratulate the town on its prosperity in numbers and population and in works, I must also congratulate the town and neighbour- hood on the why in which they have come forward to meet the wants of the infirmary, because, of course, increased want means increased money, and they have nobly come forward not once, nor twice, but over and over again to meet the wants of the neighbourhood in this particular matter. I am not going to trouble you with statistics, but I want to quote one or two figures. In 1877 there were only 87 in-patients, 948 home patients and 1992 out-patients but now the paper placed before me tells me that the in-patients last year were 607, the home patients 1,441 and the out-patients no less a number than 3,652. (Applause.) So it is quite clear that at all events shows there is a want. (Hear, hear.) There is one more subject which appears in this paper which I should also like to call attention to. In 1877 there were 449 oases of accidents—which of course, mean wounds or something of the kind which require immediate attention. for it they are not immediately attended to the• result is very much worse than if they were. There were 449 in 1877 and last year there were no less than 3,769. I appeal to you all whether it is not a matter in which your help is earnestly needed. It is very often said about charities that there are two diffi- culties and the first is to get people to subscribe at all to anything, because they very often button up their pockets and money does not come out. (Laughter.) People have to be taught to give as well as to do many other good things. Even when you do find a great number of people willing to give, I am sorry to say they very often give it, I do not say to unworthy objects, but they get misled and the money which they spend does not go to the best objects which they could possibly pick out. In the case of private charities I would strongly recommend to anybody who is charit- ably disposed, if he has any doubt about any case to ' consult the Charity Organization Society in London. They will give every possible information they dare give or can give in order to lead you aright and take care you are not imposed upon. But in this matter there cannot be the smallest doubt. If you are disposed to give largely, if you can do so, give largely. If not give what you can, because the subject whioh we are treating is undoubtedly good. (Hear, hear.) We have the highest authority there can possibly be for what I am saying. What did our Saviour Christ say ? " Heal the sick." That is exactly what we are trying to do. And, then in another sentence the full meaning of which probably on this earth we shall nvver fully realize : " Who took upon Him our infirmi- ties." Bear ye one another's burdens." That is exactly what I um asking you to do aci-day. You can have no better object. It is quite clear there is a want and I do ask those who have been in the habit of giving to continue to give, and those who have never given yet to begin by giving to this excellent institution. And take my word for it, they will never repent there- after. I have great pleasure in receiving this key as a token of the good work which is being done in this town where I was so long a citizen, and I now have great pleasure in opening the new buildings. (Applause) Lord Cross thou opened the door of the building. Mr. W. PETER IlvLasns then proposed a vote of thanks to " our old Warrington citizen" Lord Cross,

for coming there and showing his continued interest in the well-being of the town by opening those buildings. It must be pleasing to him to pat the eoping stone on the work which be commenced thirty years ago in opening the old buildiny. and his Lordship must have adequate satisfaction in seeing that in the meantime Warrington had not lost a sense of the duty it owed to those who were injured, and that the people were prepared to go on and nrovide further accommodation when it was required. (Applause.) Lord Cross had appealed to them in a manner which he (Mr. Rylands) hoped would touch the hearts of all. He (Mr. 'glands) had been appealing for two years. and he had now to thank them for the kind support they had given to the Board of Management in providing tke money necessary to erect that building. (Applause.) Rev. H. V. PIIIIOT seconded. He said that was a very Important day in the history ef the institution and indeed of Warrington. They were delighted when they received the kind promise of Lord Cross to open the extension, and they were especially grateful for the beautiful day be had brought with him. (Applause.) The vote was carried with cheers. Lord CROSS in reply said that if they thoneht any- thine of his coming there, he hoped they would follow his advice and open their purse strings. The proceedings then closed with the National Anthem and Lord Cross and other visitors made an inspection of the new premises. LUNCHEON AT THE DRILL HALL.

Mr. JOIN WHITE, in proposing this toast, said he thought they ought to take every opportunity they could of drinking each other's healths, because it appeared to him the time would shortly come when they would not have to drink at all, or even if they were allowed to drink they might not have the money to pay for it. (Laughter.) It was a source of great satisfaction that in spite of the difference of religious opinion which was very much to be regretted, there was a kindly feeling existing amongst all ministers of different religions in the town which prompted them on all occasions to co-operate for the good of the com- munity whether it was in acting on any publie, body ol• whether it was in private spheres of work, and the pnblic must appreciate the good service they were doing. (Apnlause.) The Rev. E. C. E. CARLATON in responding said the Church at present existed in very troublous times and it was not altogether easy to see one's way and one's duty. fie thought, however, the clergy were resolved to do their duty at all costs. The Church was on her trial, be believed, but with the help and support of their laity she would come well out of the crisis. (Applause). Father WHITTLE also responded and expressed the gratitude of his fellow-Catholics for what, had been done for them by the Infirmary. It was proper that that toast should be honoured because it was one of the great duties or the clergy to look after the sick and dying and they were very closely connected with the work of the Infirmary. Moreover the hospital was an essentially Christian institution, for if they went back to Pagan days they found nothing of the kind. There was nothing that did so much good either spiritually or bodily as the Infirmaz yin time of sickness. (Applause). The Rev. "THE IMPERIAL FORCES."

Mr. ROBRAT PrIMPOINT proposed this toast and in showing the appropriateness of such a toast on that occasion instanced the noble work intending the sick and wounded in the army which was done by Miss Florence Nightingale who, in his opinictn, attained woman's rights fifty years ago more than woman had ever attained them since. The imperial forces re-acted on the hospitals and the hospitals re-acted on the imperial forces and the one owed a great deal to the other. Soldiers benefited from hospitals perhaps more than any other people in the community and they had therefore a right to be recognized on such an occasion as that. (AppJause.) Captain Cuosrmn, in responding, said the safety of the country was the supreme law. Let us see to it that the Navy was kept adequately strong. Let us stick to the two power standard. He would venture to quote that great epigram of Lord Charles Beresford: " Battleships are cheaper than battles." With regard to the regular army he did not think it was right that we should allow the men who served the empire to he cast adrift after having done their seven years in the army, and beg for work wherever they could find it. (Hear, hear.) The sooner we dealt with that the better. Lastly, he came to the territorial army—that great scheme which Mr. Haldane bad given them. All he would say about that was : Let them, as far as they were able, work in order to create such a patriotic spirit throughout the country that the time might come when the young British lad would feel fa much sorrow when he was rejected from the te-ritorial army through being physically unfit as did the Swiss youth to-day when be was rejected for that reason. "THE RIGHT HON. VISCOrNT CROSS, GX.13." Sir GILBERT GREENALL said he had what was perhaps the most pleasant task of the day and that was to pro- pose the health of Lord Cross. No words of his were needed to eulogise him. He was one of the most prominent political men of his day and was ever before the public eye. (Applause.) " THB HONORARY SURGEONS OP THI INSTITUTION." Mr. GIORGI DAK nq proposed this toast and said that no matter bow fine their building and appliances, they would be utterly useless unless they were well supported by an honorary board of surgeons. They had such a board, consisting of gentlemen who devoted a great of their valuable time to the work of the institution and looked after the cases with great care and skill, without fee or reward except the thanks of the community and the gratitude of the patients. (Applause) Dr. Fox in responding said he recollected the time when the number of beds occupied in the Old Infirmary in Buttermarket-street was six and when they had one house surgeon and three nurses. Then the institution progressed until the opening of the Infirmary in Kendrick-street, which provided forty beds, and to-day I with the extension in use, they could accommodate over 160 patients and they had three house surgeons and provision for a staff of twenty nurses. In these days of Socialism, instead of levelling down they would' be lifting up the poor and putting them in a position of being attended to in such a manner that very few people could do better in their own private houses. They would want at least 45,000 a year to carry out the ' work efficiently but he had confidence in the people of Warrington that the funds would be forthcoming. (Applause.)

• "THE • RCNITINCTS AND BUILDIRS." Canon STEVINSON submitted this toast and referred to the architects as the head and the builders as the bands which carried out the scheme. They had been extremely fortunate in both the architects and builders of that extension. The name of 0 wen had long been esteemed and respected in the town. (Applause.) They knew that the senior member of that firm was passing into a period of leisure and retirement—and they hoped a very long period in which he would enjoy the fruits of his past energy. They were equally confident that the rising generaticn would sustain the reputation which had been earned by the father. With regard to the builders, one always felt when people said Mr. Davenport bad the work i n hand that it would be well done. (Applause). Mr. SEGAR OWE; responded and expressed his , indebtedness to the medical s'aff who had given such great assistance in furthering the scheme. He would also like to thank Mr. Hampson. the hard working secre- tary, who had riven them on all occasions the best he possibly could when they had demanded his labour. The building, as far as Mr. Davenport's work I was concerned, was perfect and they only hoped it would I prove satisfactory to all who came within its walls I (Applause.)

THE EXTENSION COMMITTEE." ANOTHER £l,OOO WSW/IRED. Aldermap FORSHAW proposed this toast and cordially congratulated the committee on the results of their noble work. They had had an excellent leader in Mr. Rylands whose efforts had been most untiring. (Applause.)

Sir GILBERT GREBNALL then presented Mr. Rylands with a memento of the occasion. in the shape of a replica of the gold key presented to Lord Cross, as a mark of the appreciation ani versall v felt for his untiring efforts on behalf of the institution. Whatever Mr. Ryland s took up, he left no stone unturned to bring to a successful issue. (Applause.) Mr. RYLANDS, having returned thanks for the gift, said that was a very proud day for the extension com- mittee when they looked back upon the two years of work which bad resulted in the erection of those bulldings—a monument of which they, and indeed all Warrington, might he proud. It was an added plersure to think that the sum which they said in the first linstance would be required had actually been reached on the day of the opening ceremony. (Applause). The only source of regret lay in the fact that owing to certain difficulties that were not foreseen—requirements which ware most properly put forward by the medical staff—it had been necessary to make certain alterations and extensions in old buildings which were not contemplated in the original estimates, so that another £l,OOO was still required. Therefore the sub- scription list was not yet closed and it would be a great pleasure to think that the friends of Warrington wouid come forward and complete the £16,000. "HIS WORSHIP THE MYYOR." A GENEROUS OFFER. _

Mr. W. H. BLECKLY, in proposing this toast, said that from the early days when Mr. Beamont was the first Mayor of the town to the present time when the welfare of the Borough was in Alderman Smethurst's. keeping, Warrington bad been froured with a succes- sion or Mavots of a horn any town might be proud. (Hear, hear.) The e,esent Mayor had a very persuasive tongue, and perhaps his crowning feat as a lightning beggar—(lsughter)—was in the case of the District Nursing Association when he collected the whole of the sum required almost before many of them had had time to appreciate how much was wanted. (Auplause.) They were proud to number him amongst the 'lgen who "do good by stealth and blush to find it fame." But not only in works of charity did he shine. They all knew be was a good all-round business man and watched the interests of the town in an exemplary manner. His character as Mayor might be summed up in the well- known couplet : He knows what's what.

And that's As hieh as metaphysic wit can fly. Langhter and applause.)

The MAYOR in. 'responding, said the extension scheme had bran near to his heat t ever since he became Mayor and he had hoped that the institution would be opened entirely free from debt. However.they must be thank- ful that they had raised £15,000. (Hear, hear.) He appealed to his hearers not to allow the institution to rest under the ban of debt but as Englishmen to open their hearts and try in a few months to raise the money. When the time came that Mr. ltylands was able to say "I have now got the subscriptions and want your cheque" he (the Mayor) would be happy to send hire .£25 and he hoped it would not be a month before Mr.T.tylands could write that IPtter. (Applause.)

LORD CROSS AT STOCKTON HEATH.

fl. Before proceeding to the Tofirmary, Lord Cross went to Stockton Heath where he planted a tree in Victoria Square. Amongst those present were : he Rev, and Hon. C. P. Cross, Sir Gilbert and Lady GreenalL Dr. Parker, Messrs.A. Fawcett, H. Austin, D. A. Watson, F. Rose, C. Plinston, W. H. Woodcock, E. Bradshaw, Vera B. Davies and Mrs. Davies. Mr. PLINSTON said the Garden Society felt a deep debt of g,atitude to Viscount Cross for coming amongst them. Trees would also be planted by Lady Greenall and Mrs. Hewitt.

Lori Cross and the two ladies then planted a tree after which three cheers were given. Mr. PIA ASTON proposed a vote of thanks to Lord Cross. Mr. WOODCOCK seconded and the motion wra carried unanimously. Miss DAISY FAWCETT then presented Lady Grcenall with a beautiful bouquet. Lord CRoss said he had known Stockton Heath for more than GO years. lie suggested that instead of Olirg it Stockton Eleath they should call it the Garden City of Stockton. (Applause.) PRESENTATION OF CHILDREN'S COT.

The children's cot provided out of the proceeds of. the recent highly successful juvenile ball was formally presented to the Infirmary by the Mayoress on tiaturday afternoon. The ceremony took place at a well-attended meeting of the Ladies' Juvenile Ball Committee held at the Institution under the presidency of the Mayoress. Tnere were also present :—The Mayor, the Rev. H. V. Pigot (chairman of the Board of Management), and Mr. J. If. Smethurst (hon. secretary to the committee). The Rev. 11. V. PICOT said nothing could be more appropriate than that the Mayoress should formally make the presentation of the cot. He had known her for a considerable number of 'years and knew the interest she took in children. (Applause.) The ladies and gentlemen present proceeded to the I3eamont Ward where the MAvouEss said she had very great pleasure, in the name of the Ladies' Committee and herself; in formally presenting the cot. The Bev. H. V. PIGOT said he had much pleesure, as, chairman of the Board of Management, in formally I accepting it. They would look upon it as a fulfilment of a very happy idea on the part of the Ladies' Com- mittee and their excellent hon. secretary, and be could assure them that the gift was highly appreciated. (Hear, hear.) The MAYOR replied on behalf of the Mayoress, and thanked Mr. l'igot very much for his kind remarks. He stated that he had great pleasure in contributing .C. 3 to the endowment fund of the cot. (Applause.) The ladies and gentlemen then inspected the ner wards and operating theatre.

"My little girl had rashes coming on her at times like nettle rash, since she had vaccination on her arm. They would rise at times and she would do nothing but scratch them until they blettil. WEDNESD&Y Was parti- THE BREWERY cularly notable as a land- SHARE MARKET. mark in the history of the campaign against the Licens- ing Bill. The City of London, that exclusive portion of the City of London which is the business centre of the world, was speaking with two voices. In the Aucton Itaolll it was sending up the price of Biss's shares, and at the Cannon-street Hotel it was denouncing the Government for bringing in a measure wnich spells ruin to the brewery interest. Ls eagerness to buy shares in the Bass Company is a lair measure, we take it, of the sincerity and conviction of the loud outcry about 4' robbery," "confiscation," acd what not. When the City is in a business mood, it no more believes in its own declarivions as to the inevitable effects. of the L'oensing Bill than those who are convinced of the justice and necessity of the measure, Lord BURTON, who is the central figure of the Bass concern, protested the other day that the measure for which Mr. ASQUITH has made himself responsible would coat his company two millions of Pounds sterling, and would, in fact, mean nothing less than ultimate ruin. Nhen his figures were challenged, it was shown that his company would be able to pay a dividend of per cent., even if the statement about the two millions were true.lle retired from the discussion on the ground that he did not choose to discuss the private affairs of his

arm. N,,w, supposing Lard BURTON to be justified in the view which he takes of the probable and, indeed certain t fleets of the Licensing Bill t'r rinnposing that what he said were to be taken ,Berieu'l3' by men of business, whether it he justi- nr t, what he said on the subject must have tended greatly to reduce the price of shares. Nobody with a modicum of commonsense is likely to buy something the value of which is about to be reduced to zero. Obviously, however, it has been assumed that BOCCI BURTON WREI " taikiog through his hat,"- as a rather vulgar but useful colloquialism has it. If the introduction of the Licensing Bill has had any effect on B t3B'S shares, it has been of a contrary character to that which Was predicted. In shor:, the shares have gone up instead of going down. The simple fact brought to light in the City of London on Wednesday is worth a bushel of .argu- teem. Wilateve; may he the case with other com- panies of a similar character, Bass's shares have evidently touched bottom, and they touched bottom, not in consequence of the introduction of the ',Tensing Bill, but some time before the Licensing 8.11 was introduced. •It is being represented that brewery shares are at a low price be cause of contemplated legislation. Tnat• is all impudent travesty of fact. Toe shares in breweries have been going down for years pestond R°lng down continuously. The fall began almost immediately on the formation of some of the companies, for the sound reason that in the °Pinion of tbe niarket the purchasers of shares had Paid far too heavy a price for theie whistle, as is the ease beyond all question. At the Cannon-street meeting, a letter was read from the Bisuor of CHICHE,TER, in which that Prelate said that the Bill would spell ruin for thousands of innocent persons whose money is invested in brewery companies. He is clearly I?rOtig. The Bill has not depressed the price of Shares. There was, however, deep depression in the price of shares before the Bill was brought in. This is because the shares had cost a great deal tnote than they were worth. We recommend the tumor of CHICHReTEIt to make inquiry as to who got the money. He may then find reason to change the direction of his denunciation.

* General regret will be felt in Poor Law circles at the impending retirement of Mr. J. C. Sutton, the res- pected clerk to the Warrington Board of Guardians. Mr. Sutton has had a long and valuable experience of Poor Law administration and is recognized throughout the country as an expert. He has held the clerkship to the Warrinaton Board for thirty-six years and prior to that period he was a very active member of the authority and was honoured by election to the chair. The best wishes of his fellow townsmen will go forth to him in his well-earned retirement. The Board is also to lose the services of Mr. S.Sutton,the assistant clerk, whose knowledge of Poor Law and of the history of the Warrington Union in particular has been of much service in the conduct of the business. Much sympathy is felt with Mr. Sutton in the fact that ill-health makes it necessary for him to relinquish the duties. „, *

There were humorous elements in the successful I plot of the suffragist leaders to interrupt Ur. Asquith lat the Manchester banquet on Friday night which I have not been noticed. Very elaborate precautions had been taken to guard against any invasion cf suffragists. A vigilant watch was kept at all the I principal doors of the Midland Hotel. Porters were anxiously scanning the pavement outside for hours and narrowly observing every woman who approached. More care could hardly have been taken to protect a Royal personage from bombs. And all the time in the front row of the gallery, among the wives and I friends of the diners,sat calmly two of the best-known I suffragist leaders in the country —one of them, the interrupter, in evening dress. The expression of mingled amusement and surprise with which Mr. Asquith looked up to the gallery when he heard this lady's question was a study. It seemed to say, " Even here! "

Teursday was the crowning THE INFinmeny day of the great undertaking ExTxxstori. which has loomed so largely in the public eye for the past two years—the Infirmary Extension. Under ttAttiappiest auspices the flue block of buildings ~h will henceforth he devoted to the treatment of the siek and suffering was formally opened, aid the Chairman of the Committee had the pleasure of announcing that the £15,000 originally appealed for had been raised. The present, however, is not a time for rest from laboura in tnis noble cane. Another £l,OOO is rEquired to defray the cost of unforseen alterations to the old building and the i Mayor with that public spirit so characteristic of I him promptly set the ball rolliog by offering to I give £25. This was soon followed by a subscrip- tion of a similar amount from Lady Greenall and we hope that -In a very short time toe institution will he entirely free from debt. The Committee made all excellent selection in inviting Lord Cross to open the exsension,for in performing the ceremony his lordThip was putting the coping stone on the work which he commenced over thirty veers ago when he opened the old Infirmary For one of his advanced years—his Ltirdsh!p is 85 in May —L ird Oross is remarkably hale and hearty and his speeches were delivered in a much stronger voice than is possessed by many younger men. Lord Cross was one of the most prominent political figures of his day, leaping to fame hy defeating the late Mr. Gladstone in SAith-West Lancashire. He afterwards held the officea of Home Secretary (1874-80) and Secretary for India (1886-92). His Lordship also enjoys a civil pension from the State of £2,000 per minute.

* * * It is reassuring to learn from Dr. Dawson Burns's annual review of the United Kingdom's Drink Bill that, although we spent £590,000 more on intoxicants last year than in 1906, the average expenditure per head fell from £3 16s. 1140 £3 15s. 9d. There was an increase of £973,408 in the spirit bill ; but to set against this there was a decrease on beer of £394,881. Although the rate of decrease of recent years, which in one year (1905) amounted to £5,500,000, has not been maintained in 1907, it is gratifying to know that we are spending considerably less per capita, on intoxicants than a generation ago. From 1872 to 1878 the average expenditure never fell below £4 a head, and in 1876 it touched £4 9s. On the other hand, from 1811 to 1851 (both years included) it never rose as high as £3, while in 1642 it fell as low as £2 Bs. 51d , or less than two-thirds its present amount. As matters are, we stand exactly where we stood in 1866, when the expenditure per head on alcoholic liquors was, as last year, £3 15s. 9d. * " The inhumanity of woman " has provoked a vigorous outburst from the militant Vicar of Burton- wood in this month's issue of his parish monthly. The rev, gentleman had ever a cogent way of putting things, and this is how he speaks of the cruelty of headgear : —Above a really pretty face, and resting on a well poised bead one too commonly sees the small dead carcass of a humming bird, or bird of paradise, or it may be, the wing or plume of a bird of larger species. Vanity must indeed be strong and powerful when irmoves a woman to carry on her head wherever she goes in the outer world, and to fancy herself as she goes, the dead body of a wonderfully made and beautifully coloured bird. A woman's hat should never be an open tomb, nor a woman's bonnet a portable cemetery. Dead birds, wings, osprey plumes should be tabooed by every woman, who is a woman, as vulgar, barbarous, and ferocious. Only feathers that are shed or cast naturally,can consistently be worn by any woman claiming to possess a woman's heart. * * * The immense crowds that flock to our football grounds to " assist," by their presence, in the success of the game, form the °Neat of the reproachful, and sometimes even the wrathful, outpourings af both the moralist and the physicist. A correspondent writes to urge that there is something to be said on the other side. It is, he observes, the common thing to heap scorn on the taste which leads people to flock in thousands to a football field, to pay their sixpence or shilling for admission, and then to stand for an hour and a half or two hours while a number of hired players hesport themselves in a pastime, the issue of which is rewarded as of the most profound moment. The people who go to watch football matches, it is loftily urged, would be better occupied playing themselves, or taking some other form of active physical exercise. Those who speak in this way, however, are rather apt to overlook the limited opportunities which adults have in winter time of themselves engaging in physical exercises out- sick, that of walking ; and is there not some physical effort demanded in getting to and from the field and supporting the ordeal of a ninety minutes sojourn there? The people are, at any rate, in the open air. It is possible also to value too lightly the effects of a keenly-contested football match, and the pursuit of the fortunes of a particular football team, as a means of taking people out of themselves, and direrting their thoughts for the time being from the more humdrum affairs of life. There are far less wholesome interests than that arising out of football, even if one has to be content to be a mere spectator of the game.

" Women," says Joe Pon- THE MYTERY OF aley in" The Subjection of WOMAN. K.!zia," " he nv.zdy things." Joe is like many other men. To him the mind of Ktzia is a mystery into , which he has tried to probe with limited masculine intelligence and failed. Dr. Claye-Shaw, the well- kno%l ti specialist, has been explaining at the Institute of Hygiene that the psychology of women must always remain a pAradox to men. He con- cedes to the suffragettes that the alleged intellectual inferiority of women to men is- CO exploded fable. There is mental difference between man and wornan ! which is. influenced by environment and education. But it is intellectual unlikeness, not inferiority, , that marks the female sex •fro!,» the male. Dr. ! Claye-Shaw is not, however, disposed to regard the dominance of woman over man—a condition of society which he conceives to be by no means impossible—with any satisfaction. He looks upon the natural instinct of the man to treat the woman with consideration and reverance, and to regard her as a mystery which he cannot penetrate, as. something which should not be lightly discarded by women. Man has looked upon woman as something to be worshipped so long that the claim to equality of power and similarity of opportunity in the world Comes as a disillusionment. " Surely," he says, " women will hesitate before they exchange the invincible armour of mystery and charm for the naked and tame theory of equality land

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

Blackburn last year spent 1,531,030 in drink, according to the annual returns of Mr. W. E. Moss, a local temperance misaioner, this working out at the rate of £10,212 per week and £1,454 per day.

* The " pubs," we hear, are full of petitions against the Licensing Bill. A. point worth noting is this. Many customers—thirsty and otherwise—call at many "pubs " and the same men sign different petitions at each house they call at. Thus the number of signa- tories swells. We hear of one man who will, by now, have signed about 20 petitions as his business takes him to those many public-houses.

* * * Fashionable furs come under the ban of the Rev. A. M. Mitchell, Vicar of Bnrtonwood, in his parish Magazine article on "The inhumanity of woman." Why, he asks, does not "my lady" who eats the mutton, wear the sheep-skin? and he answers his query with the statement that the skins of creatures killed for food are not tich and rare enough. "It is incomprehensible," he says "how women can, and will persist, in spite of all that has been written, too, in wrapping themselves up warm in garments dyed in blood. Those tell-tale skins! Their secret cannot be lid." Seieral thousand shares of Bass and Co. were sold at Tokenttouse-yard, London, on Wednesday afternoon and the prices realised show how unfounded, is the cry that has been raised about the effect of the Licensing Bill on brewery shares. The average peice was 40s. per share some lots being sold at 41s. A similar sale took place several weeks ago, before the introduction of the Licensing Bill, and the prices then ranged from 395. to 435. per share. It is clear, there- fore, that the bill has not had any material effect on the market value of these shares.

* * * The members forming the Government in our local Parliamentary Debating Society, to say the least of it, have the courage of their opinions. They not only brought in and passed an Old-Age Pensions Bill, giving pensions to all persons over 65 years of age, but have now tackled the Education question in a very bold and comprehensive manner. They have laid on the table of the House this week a Secular Bill. The introducer of the measurer is Mr. S. W. Jeffery who on Educational matters is somewhat of an expert. He is well-known as the assistant master at the Wycliffe Day Schools. WHITECROSS WORKS.

Employees' Recreation Scheme.

I Recreation schemes are very much to the fore just ; now, and the latest local undertaking of the kind has I been inaugurated by the employees of the Whitecross Co., Ltd. They have formed a Recreation and Social Club, which will be maintained by means of small weekly contributions, and have purchased the large house known as Meda Bank in Green-street. Work- men are now busily engaged in transforming the building into club premises, and it is hoped that the opening will take place on Easter Saturday. There will be billiard tables, library, card rooms, and the usual club accommodation, and a bowling green is to be laid at the back of the house. The Green-street scheme, however, does not form the limit of the club's activities, for it is intended, as soon as opportunity presents, to acquire a recreation ground where facilities for all kinds of games will be provided. Mr. W. Carson has been elected president of the club and numerous members of the staff have undertaken the offices of vice-presidents. Mr. J. W. Bouchier is the treasurer, and Messrs. A. Range and A. C. Binks are the joint secretaries.

This vessel will be a sister ship to the " Copenhagen " and constructed by the same builders, Messrs. PUBLIC BENEFIT—UNDER THE CLOCK FOR BEST BOOTS IN LANCASHIRE.—Advt. Parent Assaults Schoolmaster.

- At the Warrington Borough Court, yesterday' William Bibby, 375, Winwick-road, was summoned for assaulting William Mason, headmaster at the Beamont Council School, on February 28th. —Mr. A. T. Hallaway, Deputy Town Clerk, prosecuted, and stated that on the afternoon in question the defendant came to the school and complained of one of his boys having been thrashed. He became very threatening and commenced to swear and shout. Mr. Mason tried i to quieten the defendant, but he struck up a fighting I attitude and pushed Mr. Mason away. After some I difficulty he managed to get him out of the school.— After hearing the evidence the magistrates imposed a fine of 20s. including costs. On Tuesday afternoon the body of Robert Taylor, widower, aged 74, who lodged at. 3, Hoyle-street, was found floating in the Mersey opposite Litton's Mill, Quay Fold, and it was recovered by Sergeant Hesp and P.C. Woolacott. From the evidence given at the inquest which was held on Thursday by the Deputy Coroner (Mr. F. A. Jones) it appears that deceased had latterly been living with Herbert Caldwell at 7, Litton's Mill: On the night of February 13th he was in the house under the influence of drink and made some remark about certain insurance policies being "quite enough to bury him." He lay down on the sofa and Caldwell covered him up and retired to bed. When he got up deceased nad disappeared and nothing was seen of him until his body was found in the river by a flatman named John Albert Stringer.---A verdict of "Found drowned" was returned. Lloyd-Jones, the Flintshire coroner, held an I inquest at Saltney (in Flintshire) Police Station, on Thursday afternoon, concerning the death of George Richardson, carter, who lived in Legh- street, Warrington, and who met his death instantaneously at Mold Junction on Wednesday morning —Mr. E. Brassey, solicitor, Chester, appeared for deceased's employer (Mr. J. Lunt, furniture remover, Warrington), and Mr. Steel, solicitor, Warring- ton, represented the deceased's relatives.— George Hughes,licensee of the Liverpool Arms,Saltney said he was standing on Saltney Ferry bridge about 10.20 a.m. on Wednesday, when Richardson passed him with the furniture van. He was then walking by the shaft horse. A few minutes afterwards a woman shouted to witness that a man was dying, and some distance along the road witne3s saw Richardson lying on the road, having been lain over, while the van had gone on a considerable distance. When witness got up to him the man was dreadfully mangled, and was breathing his last. Another witness said he saw deceased sitting on the shafts prior to the accident. Tbe jury returned a verdict of " Accidental death." Finest DAisirsti BUTTER, Is. 2d. lb. at ASHLEY'S.—Advt. 1 ROYAL COURT THEATRE. ---

"The English Rose"

"The English Rose " is one of those exciting dramas which are always welcomed by the multitnde,and the popular parts of the theatre have again been well filled this week. The authors, - George R. Sims and ; Robert Buchanan -have woven a story of absorbing interest, the scene being laid in a beautiful but very " distressful " part of Ireland. " The English ! Rose " is the name bestowed upon the winsome niece ! of Sir Philip Kingston, a tyrannical landlord who 1; ! regarded as a usurper, and is held in hatred by t peasantry. She is wooed and won by Harry O'Mailld2 ; a cheery and popular son of the Emerald Isle, and It ; is at this point that the fierce resentment of the villain, Captain Macdmnell, Sir Philip's agent, is aroused. He cunningly incites some of the oppressed tenantry to murder Sir Philip at the Devil's Bridge, Connemara, and then saddles Harry oMailley with • the crime. Black as is the outlook against the young lover, complete exoneration comes at the eleventh hour by the dying confession of the murderer, and retribution falls upon Captain Macdonnell for his instigation of the outrage. The play is capably I presented by Nlessrs. W. H. Savory and A.. S. Pigott's company. Mr. Oswald Cray soon establishes himself as a general favourite by his forceful acting as Harry O'Hailley, for he has a fine stage presence, and is in every way well fitted for the part. Miss Violet Fare- brother also gains high honours as Ethel Kingston, ! her impersonation, especially in the lighter moods, being quite natural, and forming no small factor in the ; success of the production. Mr. Jackson Wilcox does full j justice to the unenviable role of Captain Macdonnell I who pursues the hero and heroine with the relentless- ness characteristic of stage villains. Miss Margaret Damer's version of Bridget O'Mara is marked by fitting restraint, and Mr. Ellerslie Pyne is highly ' dramatic as the luckless Randal O'Hara. Some ! capital comic relief is provided by Mr. C. Young ! Fixott (Sergeant O'Reilly) and Miss Ella Seton (Louisa Ferguson) whose dancing delights the audience. The mounting of the play is all that could be desired, the scene depicting the Devil's Bridge and ; its romantic surroundings being one of the most i beautiful stage pictures imaginable. NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME.—MR. F. R. BENSON'S COMPANY.

Mr. F. R. Benson's celebrated North Company will pay their usual annual visit to the Royal Court Theatre next week. This has always proved such a popular and successful attraction that patrons are advised to book their seats early in order to avoid disappointment. Already a large number cf seats have been booked. The follow- ing seven plays will be presented : —Monday, " The Taming of the Shrew " ; Tuesday, "Julius Cmsar Wednesday, "Twelfth Night " ; Thursday, "The Merry Wives of Windsor " ; Friday, "She stoops to conquer " ; Saturday (matinee), "Henry the Fifth " ; Saturday (evening), "Macbeth." Mr. Heery Herbert will again be seen in the leading iole, and Miss Emil Leslie and Miss Gladys Venderzee share the principal lady parts. The Company numbers 30 first-class artistes, and its popularity locally ensures crowded houses at each performance. Special low rates will be offered to school parties for the matinee.

===== 0002601_19080321_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
CONCERT IN IN PARR HALL.

Railway Benevolent Institution

The concert given in Parr Hall on Saturday evening on behalf of the funds of the above institution was noteworthy for its abundance of good vocal music sung by capable singers. The two brothers Messrs. G-. H. and J. H. Ditchburn and the daughter of one of them, Miss Winifred Ditchburn, took the burden of the concert on themselves, and very satisfactorily discharged it. The•first two are curiously alike in their bass voices and their style, and moreover they Sang songs also of such near kinship that one was sometimes not a little bothered to identify the singer. But as both were good the distinction mattered little and the " Four jolly sailormen " of the one was so closely matched by " My old shako " of the other, that when both came together in the quaint duet "Two proper men," the balance of merit was indeed found to be so evenly adjusted that the audience, who were for a considerable part of the evening coldly unmoved, grew enthusiastically insistent upon an encore. Miss Ditchburn possesses an agreeable contralto voice, which she used with taste and feeling in Barnby's pathetic little song story " When the flowing tide comes in," and in the homelier song "My am n folk." But she was obviously at her best in that bright conceit "Where are you going to,tny pretty maid ? " a duet in which she was joined by Mr. G. H. Ditchburn, to the great enjoyment of the audience. Madame A.Croxton's clear soprano and distinct though not quite per- fect enunciation enabled her to interpret most success- fully "She wander'd down the mountain side," a song of sterling merit to seldom heard in the concert-room. In the Waltz Song which Mde. Croxton sang later in the evening one would have preferred a fuller measure of that abandon which should more than anything else characterise its rendering. Nevertheless the vocalist imparted a piquancy and charm to the song which lifted her interpretation of it much above the common-place. Of Mr. J. H. Draper's tenor songs "Thom " and " My Queen "it is due to say that they evinced good taste and an intelligent grasp of the spirit and sentiment in both cases. Of the two "My Queen" was the more satisfactory performance and but for a too evident effort in taking the upper notes little was here left to be desired. The trio " Queen of the Night " (Smart), in which Mde. Croston, Miss Ditchburn and Mr. G. 11. Ditchburn took part, was one of the vocal gems of the evening. Mr. F. M. Darby, Mus. Bac., opened the concert with a neatly executed and dashing rendering of Godard's Mazurka in B flat, playing also the accompaniments to the , various songs skilfully and with excellent judgment.

ST. HELENS.

FUNERAL OF COLONEL PILEINOTON. IMPRESSIVE SCENE.

I The funeral of Colonel Richard Pilkington, C. 8., who ' for many years represented the Newton Division of Lancashire in Parliament, took place on Monday at St. Helens, and it was made the most remarkable demonstration of regard ever witnessed in the town. Most of the shops were closed, blinds were everywhere drawn, and the route to the cemetery was lined by thousands of people,who thus testified the esteem for the memory one who had done so much for the wellbeing of the borough. Prior to the funeral service a special meet- ing of the Town Council and the Education Committee was held in the T.)wri Hall, under the presidency of the Mayor (Alderman Martin), who,after personally testify- ing to Colonel Pilkington's high character and devoted ' service to the community, moved a resolution testify- ing to the loss which they and the borough have sus- tamed in the death of one who for more than thirty-five years as Mayor, alderman, and councillor has unselfishly devoted himself to the public service and to the furtherance of every good and useful work.—Aldermi Massey seconded the proposition, which was supporten by Councillors Collins, Barton, Fletcher, aud J Phythian, and Mr. Slater, a member of the Education Committee, and carried unanimously. The members of the Council and Education Com- mittee, with the magistrates and Corporation officials, then walked in procession to the Congregational Church, where they look part in the funeral service conducted by the Rev. J. Gwilym Jones, pastor. The remains—which were in the coffin of panelled oak, enshrouded in the Union Jack, surmounted by Colonel Pilkington's sword and helmet—were placed inside the Communion rails. After the service the remains were borne from the church by six sergeants of the 2nd V.B. South Lancashire ltegiment,with which Colonel Pilking- tou had a long connection, and placed on a gun carriage drawn by six horses. The procession was headed by the firing party of sixty members of the reg iment and th baud, and escorted by the mounted police* The mourners were Messrs. Arthur, Lionel, Charles W; Norman, and Guy Pilkington, sons ; Colonel W. IV' Pilkington, Mr. Cecil Pilkingtoa, Messrs. Arthie Sinclair (Southport), Edward Lawrence, and Charle. Pilkington (Manchester), lhomas Pilkington (London.. , Mr. Allan Pilkingtou, Sir Henry Seton-l(arr, Messr, Grant Wilson Vernon Roberts Arthur Todd, Murra Todd, John Wilson, (London), Dr. Masson, Fran,) Gaskell, EL P. Hawkins, W. L. Stannard, R. J. Wilsons and W. H. Evans (Bromborough). Following the mourners came the members of the 1 2nd V.B. South Lancashire Regiment, under the com- mand of Lieutenant-Colonel Thomas, with them being Colonel Verschoyle, the Brigadier General ; the 2nd Lancashire Royal Eugineer Volunteers under the com- mand of Major R. B. Glover, and the "11 " Troop of the Lancashire Hussars Imperial Yeomanry, under Captain Dixon.

At the cemetery the s cone was a memorable one. Tnere was a very large assemblage present, and the service at the graveside, which was conducted by the Rev. J. Gwilym Jones, assisted by the ltev. J. Allatt of Newton-le-Willows, was most impressive. At its close the firing party fired three volleys over the open grave, the band playing the hymn " Days and moments quickly flying," while the buglers sounded the' last post."

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The fortnightly meeting of this Board was held at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday, Mr. T. Baxter in the chair. There were also present :—Messrs. J. Spruce, G. Egerton-Warburton, A. Greenway, E. Britch, S. Wood- ward, C.E.Linaker, W.Harrison,S. Horton,. Bretherton, C.Horton, P.Collins, S. Talbot, T. Wood,W. Winstanley, J. W. Timperley, J. Douglas, J. B. Chadwick, C. E. Linaker, jun., P. The eaimates for the half-year ending Michaelmas next were submitted by the Clerk and showed a probable expenditure for common charges of £4,933, county rate £8,059, balance and contingency £3,806 ; total £16,798. Deducting the probable balance at Lady Day of £5,813, this left an amount required from the overseers, £10,985. The assessable value of the Union was £237453 and a rate of Ild, in the £ would produce £10,874, and this rate was recommended, being an increase of id. in the £ on the corresponding period last year. _

Tee estimates were adopted. FISCALITIS FOR UNEMPLOYMENT. _

The St. Alban's Rural District Council forwarded for adoption copy of a resolution on unemployment to the effect that the question was a national concern and could only be satisfactorily dealt with on a national basis, and therefore called upon the Imperial Govern- ment to take the necessary steps to deal with the problem in a national and systematic matter "by so altering our present fiscal system that the vast quantities of manufactured articles now imported into this country from foreign competing countries that could be made by British working men shall be manufactured here so as to very considerably reduce the number of the unemployed." Further the resolution protested against all schemes for reclamation of waste land and foreshores on the ground that the acreage of land out of cultivation at the present time in the United Kingdom, is so vast as to need no additions and suggested that the establishment of farm or other colonies would help to solve the unemployed problem. The matter was referred to the Parliamentary Committee.

VISITING THE WORKHOUSE.

Mr. Norman moved that any member of the Board or the Ladies Committee should be authorized to pay casual visits to the Workhouse if they gave notice of their arrival to the Master and Matron. It did not mean that anybody could go and walk round and so make themselves busy bodies. Mr. Spruce said they received some time ago a circular letter from the Local Government Board, which stated that they had a right to visit the place and were allowed to go on their own. Mr. Davies supported the latter suggestion. Mr. C. E. Linaker, sen., opposed it.. He thought it would cause a serious difficulty. The resolution was carried.

A special meeting of the Council will be called at a future date to consular the above. DANGEROUS CHEMICAL GASES.

The Clerk read a letter from Halton Lodge, Runcorn, dated March 14th, which ran : "Dear Sir,—Damages to farm crops in this neighbourhood by chemical gases have increased so extensively during the last few years and for precise information I submitted a sample of oats grown ou the above farm in a field near Halton rood to Mr.Smithama, nalytical chemist, Liver- pool, and I endorse a copy of his certificate. The oats

5

were a good well-standing crop of the " Storm King variety and were not lived at the time of harvesting a was the case in many instances last year through th exceptionally inclement weather. Now the new inspecto under the Alkali Act has been appointed and youd Council contribute towards his remuneration the Counci will doubtless take some interest in his work and reports. I have several times similarly suffered and have approached the representatives of the works, ans' your Council would be amused if I repeated the variou suggestions they would like me to believe as to the cause of a damaged crop. Part of the land in my occupation is amongst the most susceptible and exposed in the district,being practically on a level with the tops of the works chimneys, yet %whenever a complaint was made an acknowledgement that the matter was receiving attention or a visit, usually ended the matter. At leas this was my experience. lam fully aware that these works provide the livelihood for some thousands of fatuities and in fact are the chief industry of Runcorn and Widnes and very severe restrictions for the benefit of a few would be unwise, but if some remedy, however moderate, comes within the scope of your Council's authority and the evil be slightly reduced it would be of interest to this locality for I can assure you all the claims are not bogus ones. lam sending a similar letter and enclosure to the Town Clerk of Liverpool. Yours faithfully, Arthur Once." The analyist's certificate stated : " I have made a careful chemical examination of the sample of damageu oats which you left with use eu the 4th inst., and I find ' unmistakable evidence of the presence of chemical ' fumes similar to those which are usually emitted by Alkali works. In my opinion the failure of the grains to come to maturity was due to the injury by the fumes."

The letter was ordered to be sent to the new Alkali Inspector.

A HAYDOCa HAY DISPUTE.

At Liverpool Assizes on Saturday before Mr. Justice Ridley and a common jury, a counterclaim action was brought by the defendant, Peter Wright, a hay dealer, of Haydock, against tee plautiff a farmer named Berry, of Haydock, for breach of warranty. Mr. itigby Swift appeared for the plaintiff, and Mr. Tobin, and Mr. Layton were counsel for the defendant. 'rue defendant's case, was taat on Ist November, 1907, plaintiff sold him two stacks of hay at an agreed prace of £230. Tne contract was for clover hay, but defen- dant contended that after he had paid for the hay he discovered that it was of a very inferior quality. lAccording to the current market price, Wright ought, to have been able to sell the hay for 4340, out the best, price he could get for it was £6O, and on now sued Berry for the difference—£Bo. .

The plaintiff's reply to the counterclaim was that no agreement was arrived at between the parties as to the quality of hay to be supplied, and be maintained that the hay Was of satisfactory quality, having regard to the fact that lest year had beau a bail one tor crops. Evidence was given by farmers, who said that the hay was of a satisfactory quality. Mr. Rigby Swift, on x.auatt of the plaintiff asked the jury not to believe that the hay was not good simply on the ground that %,.,rigtit made a bad bargain in re-selling it. Coaosel suggested that Vright had made a bad bargain, and now wanted to place the loss ou Berry's shoulders. Mr. 'robin said the probabilities of the case suggested that a warranty was given, and the proof that the hay was unsatistactory wiis cleirly founa la the statements I of the men who had bought it from his client.

The jury, despite a lengthy letirement were unable to agree, and they were accordingly aischarged.
