PRUDENTIAL ASSURANCE COMPANY.

Another yelr of wonderful success is reviewed in the annual repa rt of the Prudential Assurance Company a summary of which appears in our advertising columns. The unique position which this great undertaking occupies is the outcome of systematic good manage- ment and a triumph of organization. Its property has permitted of a long series of concessions and reforms, culminating in the arrangement made last year to give a share of the profits to the policyholders in the Industrial branch as well as the superintendents and agents of :he company, and on all sides this is recog- nized as the manifestation of a liberal and enlightened policy. In the ordinary branch the number of policies issued during the year was 86,080, assuring the sum of £9,156,865, and producing a new annual premium ircome of £466,557. The premiums received in this branch during the year were £1,480,377 being an increase of £189,406 over the year 1905. In ;he Industrial branch the total premiums received amounted to £6,661,631, an increase of £162,603 over the figures for 1906. It has often been noted in the past, how almost impossible it is for any great disaster to happen in these Islands, or even to many of our fellow-country- men abroad, which will not leave its record in the company's experience. Thus, the Shrewsbury railway accident involved the company in six claims,amounting to £232, whilst at a railway disaster happening at the other side of the world, at Barberton in South Africa,' four claims were paid. In the loss of the s.s.l Nicaragnan—which sailed on the Bth June last from Norfolk (Virginia) for Dublin and was never again I beard of,out of a .crew of forty-six,thirteen were assured with the Company. The epidemic of cerebio-spinal meningitis in Belfast—more commonly known as"spotted fever "—was however more fatal as far as numbers go than any accident, for in Belfast alone the company paid 207 claims from death arising from this cause. The business in both branches of the company this year constitutes a record and the concern towers more eminently in the insurance world than ever. The local superintendent is Mr. J. T. Rowland, 69, Sankey-street.

ETOCKTON HEATH GARDEN SOCIETY. - --- Spring Bulb Show.

In connection with the above Society the third annual Spring Bulb Show was held in the Church Institute, on Saturday afternoon and evening. Mrs. Roger Parr kindly consented to declare the show open and amongst those present were :—Mr. Vere B. Davies (who presided), Rev. J. Collins, Mr. W. Hickman, Mr. C. Plicston, Mr. Fawcett, secretary, Mr. E. Bradshaw and others. Music throughout the afternoon and evening was admirably supplied by the Society's orchestra. The Chairman remarked that they had an admirable secretary in Mr. Fawcett. (Hear, hear.) There was an increase in the number of exhibits. He then called upon Mrs. Parr to declare the show open. (Applause.) Mrs. Parr in declaring the show open said she could hardly believe it was their third show. She was pleased to be with them and als%to see such a large number of exhibits. She then decllred the show open. Mr. Plinston in moving a vote of thanks to Mrs. Parr for coming io declare the show open said it was her first visit to their show but he hoped it would not be the last. The Rev. J. Collins seconded and the motion was carried. Mr. Hickman proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. Davies for presiding which was seconded by Mr. E. Bradshaw. Mr. Davies responded on behalf of Mrs. Parr and himself.

The Examiner, SatuDiay March 21, 1908.

OUR COMMERCIAL SUPREMACY. BRITAIN STILL EASILY FIRST.

By L. G. Ghiozza Money, M.P.

ARTICIE

There is nothing more remarkable in the world of commerce than the ease with which the United Kingdom retains its lead. We have in round figures 40 millions of people against the 60 millions of Germany and the 80 millions of America. It would be unreasonable to suppose therefore, that'we could for ever retain commercial supremacy, for Germany and America bring against us not only numbers, but kindred race genius, while America has resources over- whelmingly greater than oars and German iir has given ten times as much attention to education as we have. Yet we are almost encouraged to hope that our lead will remain with us for long when we examine the recorded facts of experience. It is of interest to set Out the export record of the four greatest commercial nations for a period of 25 years.

The sour Leaders Compared. Here is the comparison : EXPORTS OF UNITED KINGDOM, GERMANY, UNITED STATES

AND FRANCE. United United Kingdom. Germany. States. France. Mill. £ Mill. £ Mill. Mill. g 1882 242 . 159 153 143 1890 264 166 176 150 1900 291 227 286 164 1907 426 338 395 222

The years given were all of them good years of trade, or " boom "years. It will be seen that we cone out exceedingly well in this ordeal of compari- son. We have almost doubled our expoits in 25 years. In the same period Germany has rather more than doubled her exports, but she had not so moch trade to double. Arnericl appears on the face of it to have done better than either Britain or Germany but this is not really the case. Her exports much more largely consist of natural and agricultural prc- ducts than do those of Germany and ourselves. France is a veiy bad fourth. Almost level with Germany ind America in 1882 she has only increased her expoi is by about 50 per cent, in the 25 years.

Protection as a Factor.

What has Protection had to do with these figures ? If it accounts for the very great American increase, dces it also accornt for the French increase being so stnall ? America has put on £242,003,000 of expor., trade while France has put on only £79,000,000 worJr. Or take the comparison of France and Germany. Starting from almost the same level Germany has put on £179,000,000 of trade against the £79,000,000 increase by France. In the same r 2riod, starting at a much higher level, and therefore not able so easily to make increase, we put oa £184,003,000 worth rrf export trade. If any conclusion at all is to be drawA from these results,surely it is tba we have every reason to be proud of them. It would be misleading to attribute or* satisfactory position wholly to Free Trade, but even a Protectionist must be constrained to admit that the position is exceedingly satisfactory. And in view of the larger popula- tions of our chief rivals it becomes something more than satisfactoty. We are entitled to say that the comparative position of Britatin in 1907 is betterl than we might have expected. The Balance of Trade Delusion.

We have had much difficulty in persuading Tariff "Reformers " that Ice do not pay for imports in golden sovereigns. lam not sure that many of them are not yet unconvinced. What they usually do is to make out a little sum like this :

The fact is, of course, that the balance is far more than accounted for by the earnings of our ships and the interest owing to persons in this countty by foreigners and Colonials. OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

WARRINGTON.

Ordination. On Sunday Mr. Joseph Hignett Barnes, 8.A., of Wadhant College, Oxford, was ordained deacon by the Bishop of Liverpool and licensed to St. Paul's

Warrington. Penny Popular Concerts. Another highly successful concert was held in Bold- street Wesleyan School-room on Saturday evening, a large audience attending. The chair was occillted by the Rev. T. Miller. Songs were given with effect by the Misses Davenport and Roscoe, and Messrs. 'P. Vernon, C. Sinclair and A. Pennington. A duet by Messrs. Vernon and Sinclair was well received. The humorous part of the prcv.arnine was well sustained by Mr. W. Milne. Accident to a Warrington Man. On Monday afternoon, Richard Thomas, aged 38, who resides at 10, Mason-street, Warrington, was admitted to the Victoria Infirmary, Northwich, suffering from severe bruises and shock. He was driving three horses attached to :slurry along the high road at Plunabley, and was seated on the vehicle,when an unexpected jolt threw him off. According to his I statement the lurry passed over one of his leg, but strange to say it escaped fracture. He was attended Iby Dr. Terry. ACUTE INDIGESTION causes a choking sensation. A Church Worker's gratitude to DR. CASSELL'S TABLETS.

The forms which that dread fiend indigestion assumes are many, and the choking sensation which troubled Miss Polly Hunter, of Green Lane Cottages, Tyne Dock, South Shields, undoubtedly arose from an acid and upset condition of stomach and digestive organs, and was successfully ctved by Dr. Cassell's Tablets. Miss Hunter writ-s :—" I have always been very interested in church work, which I love dearly, and t was lately troubled very much with a choking sensation in my throat which prevented me joining in the singing at the church, this being a great regret to rue. I took many medicines all to no avail, and at last I tried Dr. Cassell's Tablets. I got a sample and found that even those few did me good, and now lam quite cured, and have had no return of my trouble. I cannot praise your tablets too highly." Dr. Cassell's Tablets can be got at any chemists for 101:d. a box, and are a perfect remedy for all nerve and stomach troubles.

irY DEAR CHILDREN, I must say some of you have very curious ideas as to my appearance. You remember two or three weeks since I announced a competition for the best sketch of myself. Well, the sketches to hand are very funny. Some are of quite old men, with long grey beards and hair, and others are quite young-looking; now, the question is, which am It I want a lot more sketches sent in during the next week or two—it is giving Inc great amusement going through them.

In the efforts of sending my photograph don't forget the toys for the poor little children. I must thank William Farrar for quite a box full of toys and books. Very many thanks, indeed. I hope many more will be to hand by next week. With love, UNCLE NED.

HE PHILOSOPHICAL EARWIG. The earwig sat down on a broad lettuce leaf.t A philosopher grave was he— And the point that he pcndered (and pondered with grief) , Was the Things that Ought Never to Be. "Oh, I can't understand the ways of the world," Was the soul of his constant complaint, "For what is the use of a brush to a Fox, When he's never been taught how to paint? And what is the sense of a pen for a Pig, When he can't write a line, I declare? And what is the good of a comb to a Cock, When he never has grown any hair? "And why should the Stairs have a foot, and no leg?

(I really can't get over that!) And why shouldn't the Things that Come under one Head In cold weather put on a hat? Why is it the Cricket will never play ball, And the Grasshopper don't brty‘ his hops? And why does the Axe never ask to have bread.

Or poiatoes along with its chops? And why mayn't the Dog sail the sea in his bark? Or the Kangaroo publish his tail? ,And if she can never put on her new kids, ' What do they the poor Goat avail? Why doesn't the Elephant lock up his trunk! Or the Sun build a house with its beams? . _ _ _ _

And why does the Door never eat up its jamb? So wasteful to keep it it seems! And why does the Turkey that's dead never smile, '

When a Merry Thought's sill in its breast? And why—" He stopped short: he'd been seized by a hen, And nobody e'er heard the rest! NELLIE HORNE.

TOY COMPETITION. Dear Uncle Ned,—l think this is ,a very nice competition, and I hope the poor cihildren will enjoy the toys that the "Children's Corner" is sending in. I am sending a 'few toys, also several story books, that I have finished with; they are very good stories. Your loving nephew' WILLIAM R. Femur'.

_+___

Mirrors have been sent to Nellie Horne, L. Andrews, Gladys Knight, Ethel Holt, Maud Mobley.

SNOW IN TOWN. Nothing is quite so quiet and clean As snow that falls in the-night; And isn't it jolly to jump from bed And, find the whole world white? It lies on the arm of the lamp-post, ' Where the lighter's ladder goes; And the policeman under it beats his arm And stamps—to feel his toes. No sound there is in the snowy road From the horses' .cautious feet; And all is hushed but the pottman's knocks, Rat-tatting down the street.' Till the men come with the shovels, To clear the snow away; What a pity it is that when it falls The people won't let it stay ELSLE A. NIBLETT.

A NOVEL USE FOR BABY.

Parent: "For goodness sake children, come and help me to hunt for baby; he is not in •the room wheie I left him."

Eldest Girl: "Oh, he's all right, Mamma I We are having a beautiful time, playing the finding of Moses with him. Just come and see how lovely he looks in his basket, floating on the river!" LILIAN ANDREWS.

Dear Uncle Ned,—My greatest desire is to become one of your members. I look at the "Children's Corner" every week, and always find it very interesting. In a few weeks' time I mean to send you a piece of poetry or a story. I am in the seventh standard, and am 12 years of age. Hoping you will accept me, and send me a small pocket mirror.

LEONARD HARRISON.

FIVE LITTLE BROTHERS.

Five little brothers set out together • To journey the livelong day; In a curious carriage 141 made of leather, They hurried away, away! One big brother and three quite small, And one wee fellow, no size at all. The carriage was dark and none too roomy, And they could not move about; The five little brothers grew very gloomy, And the wee one began to pout. Till the biggest one whispered: "What do you says Let's leave the carriage and run away." So out they scampered, the five together, And off and away they sped. Whdn somebody found that leather carriage, Oh, how she did shake her head; 'Twas her little boy's shoe, as every one knows, And the five little brothers were five little toes., ETHEL HOLT.

===== 0002601_19080321_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
NICE GIRLS I

Two fair young girls sat in a dainty boudoir gazing into each other's eyes. "We are such good friends, Marcia," said one, "that we can afford to say to each other just what we think. Novift I-have such a splendid scheme. Suppose we start out to-day by telling each other the exact truth, without regard to any question of politeness."

"How perfectly lovely, Lobelia," cooed the younger girl of the two. "Let's begin right away. What do you think of me?" "I think," said Marcia, "that you are almost as pretty as you think you are, and when you don't try to be you are the most charming girl In the world."

"How awfully good of you," rejoined the other; "and do you know that when you came in I was thinking that you looked just like one of those big feather dusters which had taken a notion to walk. Only your feet are so very large that the illustration was not, quite perfect." Two dishevelled young women were taken out of that aristocratic mansion five minutes later and hurried in an ambulance to the nearest hospitaL

The merits of a new church organ were thus described: "The swell died away in a delicious suffocation, like one singing a sweet song under the bed-clothes."

Miss Prion (quoting): "Wise men make proverbs, and fools repeat them." Miss Smart (musingly): "Yes; I wonder what wise man made the one you ilia repeated."

.ilousekeeper: "You don't look as if you had washed yourself for a month." Tramp: "Please, mum, th' doctors say th' proper time to bath is is two hours after a meal, Ind I haven't had anything you call a meal in '.3ls weeks."

NOT A LADY YET.

Here is a pretty story of our Queen, which happened when she was Princess of Wales. The Princess and the Royal children were coming off the yacht when one of the little Princesees slipped on a plank. "Have a care, little lady I" said one of the sailors who was standing by. The child drew herself up, and said, haughtily, "I'm not a lady; I'm a Princess!" The Princess of Wales heard this not very polite speech of her little daughter's, and with her usual good taste, elle quickly said: " Tell the good sailor, dear, that you are not a :ady yet, but hope to be one by and by."

RUTH' ASHWORTII.

HOW THE 'FI:OWEIIi3 GROW. First the seed so tiny. Hidden from the sight; Then two pretty leaflets Struggling towards the light. Soon the bud appearing Turns into a flower, Kissed by golden sunshine, Washed by silver shower. Growing sweeter, sweeter, Every happy hour; Kissed by golden sunshine, Washed by silver shower. ETHEL HOLT.

An Englishman was once visiting,Paris, and after visiting all the places of interest, he grew exceedingly hungry. He entered a café, and tried to make the French waiter understand that he wanted beef steak and mushrooms lor his dinner. The beef steak was trantllated into French, but the visitor was at a loss what to say for mushrooms. Suddenly a bright idea struck him, and, taking out his notebook and pencil, he drew several ogjects something like mushrooms—so he thought. The tvaiter.gazed at them in absolute amazement for a rr4nute or so; then it dawned on him; he went into thb hall, and he beamed 'upon the astonished Englishman as he handed him a short, thick umbrella. S. RICHARDS.

Uncle Eb, as we used to call him, anion., lotls of gobd qualities, had one failing. He did' love good liquor; but such was the state of credit that no one would trust him. He, therefore, one day resorted to a trick to answer the great desire of his appetite. He took two case bottles, piit a quart of water in one of them, then put p. bottle in each pocket, and started for the public-house. take a quart of your gin,"' said Uncle Eb, as he placed the empty bottle on the counter. The gin was put up, and the bottle was replaced in his pocket, when Uncle Eb pulled from his purse khat at a distance might seeiff like a two-shilling piece. "This is nothing but tin, Uncle Eb," said the trader. "'Eh, now it's a two-shilling ,piece," said Uncle Eb. "It's tin," said the trader; "I shan't take it." "It's all I've got." :',Very well, you can't have the gin." Uncle Eb, with- out much demurring, pulled from his pocket the quart of water. The trader took it, poured it into the gin-barrel, and off went Uncle Eb. chuckling.

An artist was showing his neighbour, a nursery gardener, round his studio. "How do you like this picture of Adam and Eve in the Garden of Eden?" "Very much; but—" "Well, what?" "You have placed in Eve's hand a variety of apple which hais been produced only during the last twenty years."

The New School at Stockton Heath. At a meeting of the Cheshire County Education Committee on Monday, the Plans and Building Sub- committee recommended that the amended plans for the new Council School at Stockton Heath, showing provision for instruction in laundry work, be approvecl and sent to the Board of Education, and that the Runcorn Rural Administrative Sub-committee be informed that the Committee regretted that they could not approve of that resolution as to the provision for such instruction not being desirable.—This was adopted.

===== 0002601_19080321_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
1907.

A SUBTERRANEAN LAKE OF FIRE.

The most remarkable feature of the geology of Nevada is the evidence of volcanic ac- tivity everywhere visible. Often steaming springs and fumaroles are met with, shewing, according to a writer in the Engineering Magazine, that volcanic activities are still in operatiob, and unmistakably indicating that Nevada is still within the historic " Circle of Fire." It is believed that the whole of the State, as well as a large part of the Mojave Desert, in California, is underlain by a single body of molten magma.

===== 0002601_19080321_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
RADIUM IN THE EARTH.

Radium, the mysterious and fascinating element,. continues to attract numerous in- vestigators. It is probably the only element which has a journal—Le Radium—entirely devoted to the dissemination of news con- cerning it. In America Professors Eve and Adams have been investigating the rate of the disintegration, and consequent giving out of heat, of radium in the earth. They con- clude that this will be the same at depths of from forty to fifty miles as at the surface. This question of the existence of radium in the earth and the heat evolved by its spon- taneous decomposition has assumed consider- able importance in the problem of the age of the earth.

===== 0002601_19080321_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
RADIUM KEEPS THE EARTH WARM.

Certain considerations as regards the, physics of the earth a 6 a cooling globe led Lord Kelvin to conclude that it could not have been the theatre of geological opera- tions for more than about 100,000,000 years. But if there is any considerable store of radium in the earth, and if this has been continuously radiating heat in time past, it will, the Globe says, have compensated for the secular cooling, and the earth may bca vastly older than the above estimate.

===== 0002601_19080321_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
DISINTEGRATION OF MyTTEM.

Dr. Blanc, writing in Le Radium, supports Rutherford's view that the emanations of radium are due to the breaking up of ite atoms. And this once admitted the sugges- tion arises that other Clements may be break- ing up in a similar way. In fact, it seems probable that all may he doing so. The very remarkable association of certain metals in Nature is conveniently explained on the sup- position that the one has changed into the other by disintegration.

===== 0002601_19080321_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
MALLEABLE CAST IRON.

Malleable cast iron consists almost entirely of ferrite and temper carbon. It has a ten- sile strength of from 40,0001 b. to 60,0001 b. per square inch. with an elongation of from 2-1- to 5 per cent., which in special cases may go as high as S per cent., and a reduction of area of 21- to 8 per cent., and may go as high as 12 per cent. A lin. bar on supports 12in. apart should bear a load at the centre of at least 3.5001 b., and be deflected at least lin. before breaking. Thin sections should be capable of being flattened out under a hammer and bent double without cracking.

===== 0002601_19080321_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
EARTHWORMS AND SEEDS.

The dispersal of seeds, and the consequent spread of plants, is largely dependent on animal agency. And in the American. Natu- ralist Mr. Andrews indicates how the earth- worm may assist in this work. The habit of 'these creatures of draqing various Leaves into the mouths of their burrows is well I known. Dativin' in his interesting book on earthworms, pointed out how, in the larger number of cases, they judiciously drag in the I leaf by the narrow end. In America it ap- pears that they use the winged fruits of the silver maple for the purpose. Under these eonditions the seeds sprout and grow, and the spread of the tree is facilitated.

DISSEMINATION OF , FEVER GERMS. • Pasteur has shewn that the same creatures may be the active' agents in the dissemina- tion of fever germs. The microbe of splenic fever was probably the• first bacillus to be definitely identified as the cause of a disease, and it was shewn by the French savant that earthworms may be the active agents in the spread of this disease. The microbes being buried with the bodies Of the animals which have died of the disease are absorbed by the worms along with the earth. They are then brought to the surface in the worm-castings, ready to be absorbed agAin by the cattle. It has been proved experimentally that the germs are thus brought up to the surface by worms.

===== 0002601_19080321_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
THE NOSE AS A GERM - FILTER.

The researches of modern science have re- vealed many wonderful things in regard to the matters of everyday life, and particu- larly in regard to the manner of dissemina- tion and prevention of disease. None of these observations is perhaps more striking or of greater practical importance than are those bearing on the prevalence in the dust-laden air of cities and towns of the germs of many of the most serious diseases, such as tuber- culosis, grippe, diphtheria, pneumonia, and others. But it appeays that, as usual, nature has provided suitable defences against the, danger. If the act of treathing is performed nor- mally, through the nose and not the mouth, the inspired air must pass through a

SKILFULLY-DESIGNED FILTERING-MACHINE, which greatly reduces the perils from this source. The simple exterior of the human nose gives no hint of the complication of its inner construction. Its interior is, however, broken up into many devious passages and channels, so arranged that a very large sur- face of mucous membrane is exposed to each breath of incoming air on its way to the larynx and lungs. The mucous membrane is moist and warm, and the air is forced to pass over it in thin layers, so that it is not only charged with 'moisture, but is also warmed to the body heat before it enters the lower air- passages.' - But in addition to these important modifi- cations an opportunity is afforded for all sus- pended dust particles, floating germs, and so forth. to 4be deposited on thee sh clamp sur- faces, from which the natural flow of the se- cretions • promptly washes them to the ex- terior. Bat THIS BENEFICENT ACTION of the nose as a germ-filter can take place only if the inspired air passes through it,. and not the mouth. Through the mouth, on ac- count of its comparatively large size the in- coming air passes with a rush, and imme- diately strikes the tonsils, which - have been shewn to be the most vulnerable portion of the throat, and form veritable portals of entry for germs of many diseases. It is, therefore, a matter of great importance that children be taught early to breathe through the nose; so that dry, unwarmed, dust and germ laden air shall not be taken into the throat and lungs. If this were consistently done, not only more serious ailments, but also ordinary colds, would be much rarer. Children or adults who nersistentiv breathe ' through the mouth, especially when asleep, are usually sufferers from some obstruction of the nasal passages, and urgently require medical care.

===== 0002601_19080321_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
Warrington Still Winning.

The Cup-holders advanced a stage further in the Competition on Saturday when they beat Runcorn at Irwell Lane before a crowd of quite 10,000, some hundreds of whom had rushed the gates and obtained admission without paying. The returns amounted to £261. Unfortunately the weather was wet and dismal all the morning, but during the match there was no rain and the ground was not in had order apart from its natural softness. Both teams turned out as selected, but the match did not start until • (lye minutes to four o'clock owing to the congestion of the Widnes bridges and the delay there of the Warrington train. The teams and officials were :

Runcorn—Harrison, back .; Flanagan, Butterworth, Moran, and Unsworth, three-quarter backs ; Jolley and Watton, half backs ; Walker, Richardson, Padbury, Williams, Macdonold, and Wood, forwards. Warrington—Tilley, back ; Fish, Dickenson, Taylor, and Watkin, three-quarter backs ; Brooks and Lloyd, half backs ; Jenkins, Thomas, Shug ars, Belton, A. Boardman, and P. Boardman, forwards. Referee, Mr. W. McCutcheon (Oldham). Touch judges, Messrs. T. Foulkes (St. Helens) and B. Ennion (Wigan). Thomas commenced for Warrington against a slight breeze, and the home lot were soon on the attack. A weak kick in the Warrington quarter resulted in Jolley marking and placing a neat goal two minutes from the start. This early success delighted the local supporters, but the tables were soon turned. Warrington used their for ward strength to great advantage and rushed the Linnets in grand style to their own quarter. Here Lloyd smartly picked up and passing between Brooks and Dickenson to Fish saw the left-winger crash over at the corner though tackled by Flanagan and Harri- son. Thomas failed at goal. but Warrington now had a lead and played like keeping it. Tilley and Taylor both caused anxiety by mulling the ball near the Warrington line, but recovered themselves as the home front rank men rushed up. There was a hot spell in the Warrington quarter and Jolley had another shot at goal which failed. A fine run by Lloyd took play to the other end. Harrison knocked on in fielding a lofty kick, and Dickenson snapped the ball up in fine style. Before he could be tackled he threw wide to Taylor who raced clean through and over the line near the posts. The cheering at this sebond score was loud and long from the huge band of Warrington supporters. Thomas should have added a goal but only hit the upright. Runcorn played up in desperate fashion and Richardson would have scored but for a great tackle by the watch- ful Tilley. Warrington held their own to the interval when the score was Warrington 2 tries, 6 points ; Runcorn one goal, 2 points. After the interval the game was of a lively itiescrip- tion. The Runcorn custodian put in a brilliant return, and following up, got possession, and threw across to Unsworth, who was knocked over the touch-line at the corner flag by Jenkins. Brooks broke away, but was quickly brought down, and Williams got to the War- rington end with a fine dribble. Moran mulled, and almost let Warrington in, Harrison just saving. Flanagan relieved, and the game became most vigorous. The crowd broke through, and stood on the touch-line. Fish had a shot at goal, which fell short, Jolley relieving with a smart run. In the last few minutes Warrington were hard pressed. Just when a score seemed imminent Fish intercepted cleverly and to the Runcorn half, but was brought down, Warrington winning a keenly fought game. Result:—Warrington 2 tries (6 points), Runcorn 1 goal (2 points).

===== 0002601_19080321_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
DANGEROUS RUSH.

EXCITING SCFINEN AT WIDNES.

There was an exciting scene, not without danger, at the Runcorn and Widnes transporter bridge prior to the match between Warrington and Runcorn at Runcorn. Thousands of Warrington men and boys made the journey by way of Widnes. The excursion trains reached Widnes close upon each other, and a great crowd made their way to the transporter bridge in order to cross the river to Runcorn.

The bridge officials had evidently not expected any- thing like such a crush, and there were no police on duty. The waiting-rooms and enclosure were packed, and when the travelling car arrived from the Runcorn side a wild dash was made for it. All the windows of the waiting-rooms were smashed and dozens of people mounted the railings and adopted the risky expedient of jumping on board the car before it stopped. When the gates were opened the crash was tremendous and the car was filled in a few seconds. Dozens reached it through the larger gates intended for vehicular traffic, and by doing so avoided payment of the fare, but the officials were powerless to prevent them. The gates closed at last,but with great difficulty and not before many people had been severely crushed. A vendor of " Runcorn team's funeral cards " fell into the cavity between the car and the end of its dock, and for some moments his position was one of extreme danger. When rescued he was found to be only bruised and scratched, but he had lost his stock-in-trade. The gates of the old Runcorn footbridge were also rushed by the crowd and hundreds walked over without paying the toll.

===== 0002601_19080321_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
POLICE COURT SEQUEL TO THE MATCH.

No fewer than nine Warrington men were charged before the Mayor at the Widnes Police Court on Monday with having been drunk and either disorderly or incao,ble on Saturday evening. They were Daniel Crooks, 5, Stamford-street ; John Culliton, 2, William- son-square ; John Cartledge, 56, Turner-street ; James Seddon, 19, Villars-street ' • Joseph Rirkham, 30, Orford. lane ; John Starkey, 58, John-street ; William Smith, 37, Mitchell-street ; and Alfred Jackson, 62, Haydock- street.

Superintendent Strickland explained that the men bad been at Runcorn watching the match between Warrington and Runcorn, and the sweets of victory bad evidently proved too much for them. The Mayor : lam sorry that the \Carrington men in the day of their jubilation forgot themselves. • In each case the men were lined 28.6 d. and costs. All the fines were paid. One individual was 10d. short, but a "whip round " among the others obviated the necessity of his visiting Walton. Curiously enough not a single football enthusiast came under the ban of the Runcorn police.

LANCASHIRE COMBINATION. PEMBERTON V. WARRINGTON " A."

At Pemberton on Saturday. The first half was full of exciting incidents. Landor opened Warrington's score after fine passing, and Hilton landed a goal for Pemberton. Warrington combined splendidly, Naylor and Forster obtaining tries, Forster improving one. Result: Warrington "A" 11 points; Pemberton, two points.

The Examiner, Saturday, March 21, 1908.

rUbLIC v. E3REWEI:K. Licensing Bill Winning all along the line. MEETINGS, LETTERS AND RESOLUTIONS

IN SUPPORT.

TWENTY-FOUR REASONS FOR SUPPORTING THE BILL.

By H. W. Massingham.

I.—BECAUSE, the evils connected with the trade in alcohol are so great that, in the opinion of Mr. Gladstone, they exceed in this countly the combined effects of „war, famine, and pestilence. 2.—BECAUSE, eight leading representatives of the LiqUor Trade, sitting on the Peel Commission, admit that in spite of all the efforts of temperance workers, " a gigantic evil remains to be remedied, and hardly any sacrifice would be too great which would result in a marked diminution of this national degradation." It is such a" marked diminution " of this "gigantic evil " that Mr. Asquith's Bill endeavours to effect. 3.—BECAUSE, these saxie representatives of the Liquor Trade admit that the evils they describe are a " temptation inseparable " from their business, and that changes in the licensing law can diminish them. 4.—BECAUSE, the lessening of these temptations can be secured through the reduction of licences, and to assume the contrary is, in the language of the minority report of the " Peel Commission," to " go against the legislative system of 400 years." " The excessive number of public houses " says this report, is a factor of first importance in the encouragement of drunkenness." This conclusion is practically endorsed by the majority report, signed by representatives of the trade. "It is generally admitted." they say, " that the number of licences in a great many parts of England and \Vales is in excess of the requirements." The Licensing Bill will secure a progressive reduction of these excessive facilities for drinking. S.—BECAUSE, many other provisions of the Bill, which aim at removing temptations to drink, are also approved by representatives of this trade on the Peel Commission. These include the extension of the bona fide traveller's journey from three to six miles, Sunday closing in Wales, and its extension to Mon- .mouthshire, and the curtailment of Sunday hours of opening. 6.—BECAUSE, special dangers to the State arise when a traffic so full of temptations becomes a Monopoly. It interferes in all elections, habitually uses its scores of thousands of public-houses to influence votes, boasts that its trade is its politics, and that each publican can bring five voters to the poll, and thus, in ,Lord Rose- bery's words brings us," perilously near the corruption of our political system." 7. —BECAUSE, the promoters of the traffic have now openly entered the political field, threatening the Church of England with the withdrawal of their financial help, should her leaders support the Licensing Bill, and proposing the boycott of temperance tradesmen, thus bidding fair to fulfil Lord Rosebery's prophecy that "if the State does not soon control the Liquor Traffic, the Liquor traffic will control the State." The most ' tyrannical American trust has never dared to use such language and to adopt such methods. B.—BECAUSE, the Bill deals not with property that belongs to individuals and was created by them, but with values which the action of State created, and has never ()eased to control. " The Justices," say Mr. and Mrs. Sidney Webb (" The History of Liquor Licensing in England," pp. 6, 7 St 8) "had it in their power to create a valuable property,and to give it to whom they chose." This right has existed since 15.52. The exercise I of this power by the magistrates has set up the monopoly value of the annual licence. 9.—BECAUSE, this discretion to giro licences to favoured individuals, has always, under English law, carried with it the right to refuge their renewal, year by year. " Just as the magistrates could create valuable property," say Mr. and Mrs. Webb," freely endow any individual with it, so they could at any Brewster sessions, arbitrarily destroy the property that they had created, and deprive the holder of it without compensation."

lII—BECAUSE, the Bill while it proposes no such "arbitrary" action, gives the brewing trade ample notice that the State means to resume public rights to grant or to withhold licences. For 14 years the Trade remains a favoured monopoly. After that period it will have to pay the fair market price for any trading facilities it gets. Now the State is bound ; it gives notice under the Bill that it means to be free. 11.—BECAUSE, the trade has had ample notice from the law, from statesmen, and from its own legal advisers that the annual licence carried with it no vested interest. Such warning was given it by the Over Harwen decision of 1882 and the decision in Sharp v. Wakefield in 1883. In the year following the former case Mr. Nash, Counsel to the Licensed Victuallers' Association, wrote to the " Morning Advertiser" saying that "there cannot be the smallest doubt that in the strict sense no such thing as a 'vested interest ' exists and that subject to appeal, the magistrates can refuse to renew the licence of the largest, most useful, and best conducted Hotel in England." 12.—BECAUSE, Mr. Balfour himself, by the Act of 1901 destroyed any such vested interest in new licences. In introducing the measure he said : " The condemnation of the present system is that Parliament in its wisdom has created a monopoly, and given the value of that monopoly to individuals. We think one of the great reforms of the Bill is that it will in future prevent the growth of a monopoly value ; which, as you have allowed it to grow up, you cannot, in our opinion, take away without compensation, but which sniely our ancestors would have been wise never to have allowed to grow up, and which we ought to do our best to prevent growing up in future." 13.—BECAUSE, the present Bill only proposes after a reasonable interval to apply to existing licences, the principle of public freedom which Mr. Balfour established for new licences. 14.—BECAUSE, while the Bill is, in principle, fair to the brewing trade, it treats the publican better than Mr. Balfour's Bill treated him. It gives him a right to compensation for loss of profit if the licence is lost during the period of reduction. 111.—BECAUSE, it will stop the power of illegitimate competition known as the" long pull." 16.—BECAUSE it shortens the publican's hours and gives him a chance of a Sunday holiday. V.—BECAUSE it will get rid of the houses known as" tenant-traps," where no publican can earn a fair living. 18.—BECAUSE it saves the workman and his wife and family from drinking chit's, which are simply unlicensed and unregulated public-houses.

_ 19.—BECAUSE it stops the hawking of liquor from vans. 20.—BECAUSE it will keep the child out of the public-house.

21.—BECAUSE it will put an end to the anomaly of the present system of assessing compensation for extinguishing licences under which a tied house gets half as much again, and sometima twice as much, as a free house. 22.—BECAUSE it will make the basis of taxation and compensation the same, and by giving to owners of licensed property a substantial interest in making true declarations of annual value, it will secure a fairer contribution by the trade to rates and taxes. Thus it will reduce the burden of other ratepayers. 23.—BECAUSE it will give the people the same power which a great landlord now has of preventing the increase of the number of licences in a given area. 91—BECAUSE under it the community will ultimately be free to regulate the sale of liquor without regard to any consideration but the welfare of the people.

===== 0002601_19080321_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
Bishops Support the Bill.

RESOLUTIONS OF THE CHURCH OF ENGLAND

TEMPERANCE SOCIETY. A special meeting of the Central Council of the Church of England Temperance Society was held in the Hoare Memorial Hall of the Church House, West- minster, on Tuesday. A very large meeting was pre- sided over by the Archbishop of Canterbury, and attended, among others, by the Bishops of London, Manchester, Winchester, St. Albans, Birmingham, Landaff, St. Asaph, lVakefleld, Ripon, Kensington, Croydon, Stepney, Islington, Kingston, Barking, Woolwich, Colchester, and Bishops Fisher and Ingham. The following resolutions were passed,the first being proposed by the Bishop of London : "That this Council, recognising that the Licensing Bill now before the House of Commons is in the main framed on the lines advocated for many years by the Church of England Temperance Society, and embodied in bills promoted by the society, gives it in general a hearty support ; that this Council gives its hearty support in particular to the principle of a time limit for com- pensation, but should it be proved that the time limit of fourteen years will impose an unreasonable strain upon the trade, the Council would suggest such exten- sion as may be considered fair and jest : that the Council continues, as heretofore, to support the principle of the ultimate resumption by the State, on fair and equitable terms, of the full monopoly value attaching to licences as such ; that before the expiry of the time-limit licensing commissioners should submit a scheme under which a distinction should be drawn as to the treatment of old licences which survive as dis- tinguished from the new licences, but such a scheme shall not imply any vested interests or debar the State from recovering its power of control ; that the regula- tions as to clubs should be very considerably strengthened ; that restricted hours of sale on Sundays should be applied to the metropolitan area ; and that the question of the exclusion of children from public- houses should he fixed by statute?' A motion going into details in connection with the treatment of clubs was referred to the executive of the society to deal with.

===== 0002601_19080321_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
LIVERPOOL C.E.T.S. AND THE BILL.

The following resolutions were unanimously passed at the special meeting of the Executive Committee of the Liverpool Diocesan Branch of the Church of England Temperance Society held on Thursday afternoon at the C.E.T.S. Offices B. Commerce Court. " That this Executive Committee gives its hearty support to the main principles of the Licensing Bill now before the House of Commons as being in accord- ance with the proposals which the C.E.T.S. has for many years advocated, while the Committee is prepared to favour a modification of time limit, if such should be found necessary to meet the demands of Justice." "That this Committee,fearing the extension of drinking facilities through excessive granting of licences to clubs, and the danger of monopoly values arising through such action strongly deprecates the granting of club licences except under much more rigorous restric- tions than those provided in the present Licensing Bill."

===== 0002601_19080321_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
Free Church Campaign.

At their first meeting, held in London, the newly- elected committee of the National Council of Free Churches appoini.ed a special sub-committee to organise a plan of campaign to he carried on on a large scale all over the country in respect of the Licensing Bill. Meanwhile public meetings have been held by the local Nonconformist Councils, with the object of informing the public on the issues involved. Strong resolutions in favour of the Government measure have been passed by several councils and churches. In connection with the congregational Union of England and Wales, an urgent appeal is being sent out to every Congregational minister in the country in regard to the support of the Government Licensing Bill. The committee are prepared, as may be found practicable, to send deputations to explain the bill and urge that meetings be organised for the exposition of the provisions and the prospective operation of the Bill.

===== 0002601_19080321_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
Support from the Wesleyan Quarterly

Meeting.

At the Wesleyan Quarterly meeting held on Thursday a resolution in support of the 13i11 was proposed by Mr. E. J. Guest, J.P.. and carried with only one dissentient. The resolution was as follows :—" That this meeting consisting of representatives of the Wesleyan Methodist Churches of Warrington, Sankey Bridges, Latchford, Lymm, Penketh, Moore, Whitley, Antrobus, Appleton, Padgate, Martinscroft, and Stock- ton Heath, heartily welcomes the Bill brought in by the Government in favour of Temperance Reform. In the Licensing Bill they recognise the courage of the Government in their attempt to deal with a Trade which is of great power, and which is opposed to any interference with the monopoly which has unfortunately been allowed to fall into their hands. Whilst reeliFing that 14 years is a long time in which to recover this monopoly and errs on the side of generosity, they gladly accept the Bill and pledge themselves to do all in their power to aid the Government in passing it through both Houses of Parliament."

In proposing the resolution Mr. GUEST said: I have a sense of duty in proposing the resolution for your unanimous acceptance. The Government have not sought to carry favour by bringing in their Licensing Bill, on the contrary they are by so doing risking their' very existence. They are opposing perhaps the greatest power we have at the present time in the country, a power which is entrenched on evey hand and lies in ambush in every public-house. There is however I trust a greater power in our midst, a power certainly which ought to be greater, and is the power of the Kingdom of Righteousness which the churches are supposed to represent. It is at the bidding of this power that the Government has nerved itself to attempt this great feat. Having forced the hands of the Government we are I bound to give them all the support we can. We should prove ourselves cowards if we fail to hold up their hands. It would be ridiculous if it were not extremely painful to witness the effrontery of this great monopoly in declaring that this Licensing Bill is an attempt at spoliation, confiscation and robbery. In raising such a cry as this they must be bereft of all sense of justice and fair play. In their heart of hearts they must surely realise this is all bunkum. For many years they have been enjoying a monopoly which does not belong to them, and which has made them rich beyond all reason. In their greed of avarice they do not stop to consider bow many victims they have left behind them. Spoliation ! Confiscation ! Robbery ! How many property owners have been spoiled of the value of their property by the presence of Licensed houses ? How much of the hard-earned money of the people has been confiscated in rates and taxes which have been necessary to maintain prisons, workhouses and asylums to mitigate the evils of the Trade ? How many widows and orphans have been robbed of the affection and support of husbands and fathers who have fallen under the stress of temptation offered to them in such abundance? If thereshould be one here who thinks this is a political question and ought not to be intro- duced into the Church I contend that it is a political question which is distinctly ethical and shouhl appeal to all lovers of righteousness. It is an accident that it has been brought in by a Liberal Government. Whether an ethical question of this sort be brought in by a Liberal or a Conservative Government is a matter of' little moment and I rejoice to know that this Bill will have the whole hearted support of multitudes who are bitterly opposed to the Government on almost every other question. It is not desirable that I should take up your time in trying to show in what directions we think the Bill might be improved. Let us recognise the good that is in it and try to realise our obligation to accept it, in all good faith. If we fail in this it may be that we shall never have another Government willing to risk so much again. The resolution was seconded by Mr.J.G. Withinshaw, of Penketh and carried.

Meetings on the Bill

A public meeting in support of the Government Licensing Bill was held in the Mersey-street Mission room on Tuesday last. Councillor F. Stott, J.P., pre- sided. Mr. E. Boreland, U.K.A., said that this Bill was a great and good measure and its greatness and goodness would carry it through. The active opposi- tion of the trade indicated how good a measure it was for never in the history of Licensing Reform had the trade been aroused to such good activity as at the present time. The statements made by the trade were false and misleading. The great reductions as the Trade termed them were not so great as the Trade would have us believe. Since 1901 the reductions had only been 1 per cent, per annum and under the new Bill the reductions amounted to 2 per cent, per annum during the 14 years time limit. The time limit of 11 years was a fair allowance both to the Trade and the temperance party. It would give fair opportunity for a gradual reduction of licences. Tlfe cry for a longer period came from breweries whose property during the passed few years had decreased in value, and who hoped to keep open until the magistrates relieved them of their burden by awarding them compensation above the actual value of their property. The cry of the Trade that more liquor would be consumed even though ; licences were reduced Vas a false one. Brewers did not keep open houses and pay rent, rate, taxes and employees just for fun, but because they were able to sell liquor. Increased facilities meant increased drink- ing. When you reduce the facilities for the consum- tion of liquor you also reduce the Consumption. This Bill was the Englishman's charter of liberty, and every man and woman who had the welfare of the country at heart would do well to support this measure and do all in their power to make it law. A resolution in support of the Bill was moved by Mr. E. BORLAND, seconded by Mr. J. GARNER and carried unanimously.

DEMONSTRATION AGAINST THE BILL.

In another column appears an advertisement of a " monstre meeting" to be held in the Parr Hall next Thursday to protest against the Bill.

MR. HOWES TO:ADDRESS JUNIOR LIBERALS. On Monday evening next Mr. Joseph Howes will address the Junior Liberal Association oa the Licensing Bill.

"The writing on the Walls of Warrington." This was the subject of a forceful and impressive sermon by the Rev. R. E. Letheren at Golborne-street Baptist Church on Sunday evening. Announcing as his text St. Mark ii. 27., "The Sabbath was made for man and not man for the Sabbath," Mr. Letheren said that as he had been walking about Warrington that week his attention had been arrested by an unusual sight upon its walls. It was a beautitul sentence from the Bible,those very words of Christ that he had taken for his text, and one wondered how in the world that bit of sacred writing had got upon the walls of Warrington. Had the Beautiful Warringtan Society exhausted its possibili- ties of usefulness in the direction of planting trees in the streets and taken up the business of mural decora- tion seeking thus to plant beautiful thoughts in men's minds ? Or had the Religious Tract Society been left a legacy and were they spending it in thus disseminating Scripture truths so that wayfaring men who are not all fools might read it and pass on their way refreshed? Or was some piously disposed person in the gracious philanthropy of his heart seeking by this means to remind his fellow townsmen of the priceless privilege of the day of rest and exhosting them to rescue it from misuse ? But when the dazzling light of a glad surprise that first rivetted his eyes to the text, left them free to look about it, he discovered that it did not stand alone. It was but a sentence on a bill that had for its heading in type many times larger than the text "The public house a public need." Never surely since Christ Himself was crucified between two thieves had this saying of His found itself in such corn- pany ! While,continned the preacher,' protest against the skameless irreverence of such a misuse of words that the world holds sacred, I am more amazed at the insanity of the idea. It is surely the latest illustration of the ancient saying that those whom the gods are about to destroy they first drive mad ! The classical instance of the quotation of Scripture to promote tride interests is " Slaylock." And Shakespeare has for ever branded the baseness of such abuse of it :" Mark you this,Bassanio, the devil can cite Scripture for his purpose. An evil soul producing holy witness is like a villain with a smiling cheek." The brewing interest must be in a bad way indeed to be willing to walk in Shylock's shoes. This appeal of the public-house to the Bible could but disgust all decent minded people. With regard to the declaration on the poster that Sunday Closing had been a failure the testimony of public men was adduced to prove the very reverse as to Wales, Scotland, Ireland and Australia. Mr. ILetheren then proceeded to examine the drawn- stances that gave occasion to this great saying of Christ. It asserted that the Sabbath was a Divine pro- vision ; it was an appeal for its restoration to its original purpose. When the Trade takes this high ground of protest against the Bill 'insinuating that it robs man of his God-given rights by restricting a little farther the sale of drink on the Sabbath Day, one expects some show of consisteacy, and is the more amazed at the way it is addressing the Church of which its " majority are members." If that is their respect for the rights of God One knows bow much importance to attach to their appeal. The Trade first and God . . . second ? What about the rights of man? There is not a Hie of Scripture that suggests that the Sabbath was made for man to do what he likes with. It was made to be a means of helping to restore man to the image of God in which he wa.s made. Is there anything on God's earth that so distorts and destroys that image as drink ? Witnesses were quoted as to its effects on body and mind. Cardinal Manning, for 35 years priest and bishop in London said "The chief bar to the working of the Holy Spirit of God in the souls of men and women is intoxicating liquor." And wig this Trade, banned by princes, statesmen, doctors, judges, magistrates, labour leaders, that quotes Jesus Christ in support of Sunday drinking ! ! The liberty of the Sabbath ? "Is it to save life or to kill ?" The liberty of the subject ? Is it to sell drink to men and women for whom Christ died ? Listen to the cry of the children and let the Lord of the Sabbath decide who said : " Whoso shall offend one of these little ones it were better for him that a millstone were hanged about his neck and that he were drowned in the depths of the sea."

===== 0002601_19080321_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
" Second to None." REV. A. M. MITCHELL'S PRAISE.

Support from Junior Liberals. In his paper entitled " Current Events" at the ! Warrington Junior Liberal Association meeting on Monday evening, Mr. J. Derbyshire referring to the Licensing Bill, said that no one knew better than the rich brewers the power of money. They were using that power to the full now. Wherever they went, wherever they turned "robbery and confiscation' stared at them from every hoarding, assailed their ears in every public place, train, tram, or street and mostly, he regretted to say it, the Licensing Bill and its authors were spoken of in terms of abuse and derision. The war over the Bill was being waged in the most bitter, rancourous and unscrupulous manner. Absolute untruths and hitting below the belt seemed to be the order of the day. At a meeting held at the Town Hall, I London, a speaker advised everybody to boycott those tradesmen who were known to be in favour of the Bill, at the same time saying that the Bill was drafted solely to please the Nonconformists, who enjoyed nothing better then plundering the Church or the brewer. The name of that gentleman was Mr. Harold Smith, brother of Mr. F. E. Smith, of Liverpool and he (Mr. Derbyshire) believed Mr. H. Smith was announced to speak at the forthcoming brewers' meeting to be held in the Parr Hall on the 26th. There had also been a serious attempt to immediately increase the price of beer, so as to arouse the animosity and opposition to the bill, of the every day working man beer drinker. When one turned from these fierce outbursts and tactics, to the Bill itself, one became astounded at the uproar caused. The Licensing trade to-day is the only pro- tected business in the country. The recovery by the State of the monopoly value of licences and the ending of the State recognition of vested interests given by Mr. Balfour, giving the brewers fourteen years to set their house in order, seemed to him to be but bare justice to the nation as whole. The gradual acceler- ated reduction of licences and the giving back to the local bench of magistrates full discretionary powers, local option regarding new licences, the protection of tenant against the tyranny of the brewers, strict club supervision. All those clauses seemed to him to be very moderate proposals. The Bill, he concluded was worthy of the support of every well wisher of his country and fellow men. (Applause.) Mr. E. WYNNE-JONES said he was not the gentleman who proposed a vote of thanks to the directors at a meeting of the trade held recently. He did not know the man who did and he did not wish to know him. (Laughter.) If they could meet together somewhere to discuss the Bill he would endeavour to prove to him that it was one of the most progressive measures ever Introduced in the House of Commons. (Applause.) He then moved the resolution in favour of the Bill, the teift of which we have already published. He added that some of the brewery companies were making the biggest profits ever made by any trade. Sunday closing would be very beneficial indeed. They as young Liberals must do all in their power to support the Bill which he hoped would become law. (Applause.) Mr. GRAY seconded. The resolution wae supported by Mr. B. H. JONES, Mr. E. ENGLAND, Mr. ALBINSON, and the CHAIRMAN, (Councillor Hawthorn) and carried unanimously. The Bill Discussed at Penketh.

On Wednesday night the Penketh Day School was lilted by a large and representative audience to hear an explanation of the Government Licensing proposals from Mr. James Dodds, President of the Warrington Citizens' Temperance League. The chair was occupied by the Rev. E. J. Gawne, and he briefly introduced Mr. Dodds, who, having gone through the main clauses of the Bill, dealt exhaustively with the time limit pro- visions, as upon these, in his opinion, the real struggle with the drink interest would take place. He showed that,under the licensing laws until 1901,0 n the evidence of the leaders of the "trade," from which he quoted, the licensees held their licences for one year only, and these were all subject to review and consideration yearly for renewal or extension at the discretion of the magistrates. Mr. Balfour's Act of 1901 was practically a brewers' act under which they ultimately claimed they had secured a freehold interest on their licenses. This, however, was not so as Mr. Balfour on several occasions distinctly stated that nothing like a freehold interest was given by his Act.and that no future Parliament would be in any way precluded from free action in regard to the introduc- tion of a time limit. Lord Hugh Cecil said in the House of Commons that the Church of England Temperance Society supported Mr. Balfour upon this understanding, and the Arcnbishop of Canterbury sought to introduce in the House of Lords a time limit of 14 years, the same as that now proposed by the Government. There was nothing to-day to prevent the Government establishing free trade in licenses, in which case any vested interests of the brewers would be practically worthless. Clearly the monopoly values of licences were the property of the country, and subject to a reasonable time limit the -country must beve its property restored to it. Questions having been invited, and answered, and Mrs. C. E. Parker, Mr. Withinshaw, Mr. A. and Mr. J.Seed, Mr. W. Walker, Mr. Carman, Mr. Faulkner, and the Rev. E. J. Gawne having spoken in favour of the Bill, Mr. Dodds proposed the following resolution "That this public meeting heartily welcomes the Licensing Bill 213 a great measure of Temperance Reform and rejoices that it provides for the immediate applica- tion of the principle of local option to new licences and for the extension of thesame principle automatically to all licences after 14 years. We are, however, of opinion that this time limit is unduly long, and hope that the Bill will be amended in this respect. This meeting further assures the Government of its strenuous support in their efforts to pass the Bill into law. Further, that a copy of the resolution be sent to the Prime Minister, the Chancellor of the Exchequer, and Mr. J. A. Seddon, M.P. for the Division." 'the resolution was seconded by Mr. G. Carman, and a most interesting and successful meeting was shortly after brought to a close. The Briton and the Brewer. Mitchell, Vicar of Thirtonwood ' writes as foLlows on the Bill in his Parish Monthly : I" The violet outburst against the Bill has simply been engineered, and is entirely without justification, for the New Licensing Act is not ' drastic ' as the drink traffickers and half, and half temperance folk want the country to believe. The Bill is a strong one, but not drastic ; it very skilfully and thoughtfully tempers mercy with justice, and is a truly great and wise measure of social reform,`senond to none.' Nothing to equal it in Temperance Legislation has hitherto been brought before Parliament. 'Spoliation," Confiscation,' 'Robbery,' are familiar cries and have ever been the watchwords of the Trade (and its supporters), when it has been proposed to lay further restrictions upon the sale of alcoholic drink, and to bring licences more under the control of the State. A more violent out- burst of rage than usual was, therefore, only to be expected when the latest and most complete temper- ance measure saw the light. The Trade is no novice in tactics of this kind,and is past master in the art of throw- ing dust in the eyes of the people. The terms proposed by the Bill provide handsome treatment for those who have been allowed for years to build up a powerful and scandalous monopoly, and which has now become a standing menace to the country. The Bill only makes provision for the annual com- pulsory closing of 24-th houses in any. one Licensing District during the time limit. To require the surrender of a couple of licences annually during a term of 14 years in each of the 1007 Licensing Districts is surely neither robbery nor confiscation, especially when a quid pro quo will be forthcoming throughthe Com- pensation Fund. This requirement is not burdensome; in the opinion of many temperance workers 'the trade is being too much considered and is getting oft far too easily. Warrington's prospects at Broughton are not exactly gay and glittering. They would, for instance, have been much brighter at Barrow or Leigh, but they might have been worse at Hunslet or Wakefield. "That this meeting appreciates the genuine attempt made by the present Government to introduce into the Licensing Laws of this country a measure of reform which is conducive to the sobriety and future well- being of its people by the reduction of excessive facili- ties with the prospect of Local Option, under which enlightened opinion will have the power and privilege of self-protection in each locality." NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL

THE CUP-TIES. By " Ovallst." Tradition holds good, and Runcorn have once again failed to beat Warrington in a cup-tie. Time was when the Linnets were always too good for the Wire- pullers —for about fourteen years they beat them with monotonous regularity - but in that time the clubs did not meet in a knock-out competition. Twenty-one years ago they met to decide the possession of the old West Lancashire Cup, and had to adjourn to Southport to settle ; matters, and Warrington won an historic encounter. Until the Lancashire Cup-tie on October 12th last the Clubs had steered clear of one another in Cup- ties since 1887, but even in the Lancashire Cup-tie I -- Warrington triumphed with ease. Saturday's TO-DAY'S MATCH.—TEAM EXPERIMENTS. encounter in the great tournament of the season Warrington will have the novelty of a visit to-day revived old memories and aroused tremendous interest from a Welsh club—one of the new converts to in the district. All roads led to Runcorn, and most Northern Unionism, viz., Ebbw Vale. The teams of them were pretty welllined with football enthusiasts. have already met on the Welsh club's ground where Fully 10,000 spectators assembled on the primitive , Warrington gave a brilliant display and gained a I ground at Irwell-lane, and the receipts were £261. substantial victory. Two more points should naturally This was not quite a record but it might have been if accrue as the result of this afternoon's encounter. several hundred impetuous partizans had not broken I through the gates and entered the ground free. ; Opportunity is being taken to experiment with the Warrington team in order to decide upon the strongest It has been a frequent bast of late that the means , combination for next week's Cup-tie. The team lof transport and communication between Widnes selected to oppose Ebbw Vale is as follows :—Tilley, and Runcorn are now admirable. But the river . full back ; Fish, Dickenson, Taylor and Brooks, still flows between and even the two bridges three-quarters; Lloyd and Jenkins, half-backs ; proved sadly inadequate on Saturday. Poor , Thomas, Shugars, Jenkins, P. Boardman, Belton and despised Warfington Bridge would have con- Heath, forwards. veyed the crowd with ease could it have stretched across the river at this point. Most of the Warring- ton excursionists detrained at Widnes, and they together with the Widnes folk made a raid on the Transporter and the old Runcorn bridge. About 2,000 people tried to board the Transporter car at once—which was rough on the car—and some damage and delay ensued. Even the Warrington players had to wait their turn and were 25 minutes late on enter- ing the ground. But if the arrangements were inadequate here, they were farcical at the railway bridge. Nearly as many people were trying to force their way through a narrow gate, while an ancient L. and N.-W. servitor struggled vainly to collect the fares. Quite half the crowd swarmed over the fence on to the bridge pell-mell free of charge. It was a wild horde altogether that crossed from Widnes to Runcorn, but they were rewarded by an exciting match in the end.

Watkin has been called home to South Wales owing to the illness of his sister. The idea of the changes from last week is to see how Jenkins com- bines with Lloyd for the first half and then for Lloyd and Brooks to change places in the second half. There is a probability of Brooks and Jenkins being the halves for next week, much as everybody would regt et seeing Lloyd deposed. Still he is not quite the equal of Jenkins, and if the latter is strong he must have a place in the team. A. Boardman has been relegated to the "A" team. It was evident at Runcorn that he had not fully recovered from his recent illness.

The " A" team has another severe Combination engagement, with Wigan Highfield, but there is confi- dence that the selected thirteen will capture the maximum points. The team is: Bramhall, full back; Landor, Cartwright, Isherwood and Tickler, three quarters ; Daintith and Jordan, half backs ; A. Board- man, Harmer, Kettle, Forster, Jolley and Fairhurst, forwards.

Warrington's victory of 6 points to 2 was not a big one, but truth to tell the Runcorn men never looked like winning. They flattered their supporters by getting a goal in the first two minutes, but Warrington immediately scared a try, and a quarter of an hour later added another which settled the issue. There was no more scoring, but there might have been had Warrington needed further points. The match was, of course, not absolutely won when Warrington were four points ahead, but they seemed to have got the measure of the homesters and resorted more to safety tactics than would have been the case supposing Run- corn had held the lead at half time. It looked very much as if Fish could have scored another try in the last five miuntei if he had wanted, for he repeated his feat of the cup-final last April in snapping the ball near his own line and running to the other end. For some reason or other he slowed down after getting clear away and was captured tamely in the Runcorn qunter He had raised the siege and seemed content, but the Warrington supporters would gladly have seen him complete the effort in his own style.

There were only two occasions on which Runcorn came very near scoring. Once Richardson got hold and dashed for the line, but was finely tackled by Tilley just outside the rut. The other occasion was an even nearer shave for Warrington. Unsworth —a Warrington lad took advantage of a blunder by Watkin and raced away down touch with a clear course before him. He seemed certain to score, when Jenkins came up like a torpedo and rammed the Runcornian a yard from the line. It was a great save, and as creditable to the speedy Newport forward as a try. Runcorn git possession from the scrums in marvellous fashion, but they were quite out-played at centre, where Dickenson and Taylor performed prodigies of skill and valour.

The game was of the typical cup-tie order and not remarkable for brilliant combination on either side. Warrington were most convincingly superior the moment after Runcorn scored the opening goal. • They completely scattered the Runcorn forwards and carried play to the home quarter. Lloyd swung the ball out and a sample of the Warrington passing was accom- plished with celerity, Fish finally receiving and beat- ing the defence brilliantly at the corner. The second try came through Dickenson's opportunism. The Runcorn full back missed a catch, Dickenson snapped up the ball at top speed and threw wide to Taylor who left all pursuers standing. It was a bit of fine individual work and put the seal on the greatness of, the Warrington centres.

Coming to individual play, after the two men just mentioned, Tilley and Brooks were the best backs on the Warrington side. Both performed brilliantly at times and were always safe. Fish was variable, but he scored a try which no one else would have got. Watkin made one fine run on the right which was worth a try, but his old failing of hesitancy on the de?ence was again apparent and caused the Warring- ton men some anxious moments. Until he can over- come this fault his position in the Warrington team is bound to be precarious. Lloyd did very well indeed, but the forwards were either beaten hollow in the serums after the first quarter or they 'were taking things easily. In the loose they carried all before them, but at heeling nut to the halves they were distinctly second-best. Jolley was the hero of the home side as usual, and played a magnificent game, but the support of Butterworth and Moran at centre was worth nothing. Barring one fatal blunder Harrison played a capital game at full back, and he is to be sympathised with under the circumstances.

• The match was naturally very keen, but there was never a stippage for rough play. iStrangly enough quite half a dozen Runcorn men were "laid out "at one time or another, while the Warrington players all appeared to have charmed "lives." Mr. McCuteheon kept the teams well in hand and refereed very capably, though I thought he was rather lenient in regard to the knock-on rule.

The draw for the third round of the Northern Union Cup and the appointment of officials were made at a meeting of the Cup Committee, held at the Grosvenor Hotel, Manchester,on Tuesday, as follows : BARROW V. HUNSLET.—Referee, R. Robinson, Brad- ford ; touch judges, B. Enion, Wigan, and T. Foulkes, St. Helens. BROUGHTON RANGERS V. WARRINGTON.—Referee, H. Ferrer, Keighley • touch judges, G. Moore, Runcorn and J. T. Hayes, Leigh. LEIGH v. MYRTIIVR TYDvlL.—Referee, W. H. Wood, Huddersfield ; touch judges, H. Broome, Oldham, and A. E. Gregson, Salford. Hurd. V. WAKEFIELD TRlNlTY.—Referee W. McCutcheon, Oldham : touch judges, R. L. Smitl;,Brad- ford, and Tomes, York. To be played on the ground of the club first named on Saturday, March 28th.
