LOCAL PARLIAMENT.

HOME ROLE BILL PASSED. A Secular Education Bill.

The Secretary for Ireland (Mr. J. F. Awbery) may congratulate himself on having introduced the measure which has produced thp most interesting debate since our local Parliament commenced its deliberations. I There were very few dull moments from the beginning to the end of Thursday evening's debate, for a variety !of opinions found expression and all the points were ; eagerly seized upon and provoked lively manifestations of approval or dissent. It was approbriate that when Home Rule was the question of the moment, the House should be favoured with the presence of a real M.P., in I the person of Mr. Murphy, the member for Killarney, who sat with the Nationalists.

--------- • THE PREMIER AND THE NAVY. Replying to a question by the Member for Lincoln on naval matters, the PREMIER. said he had never heard ; of a "little navy" party but should it exist the Govern- ment did not intend to interfere with it, any more than they would with the Navy League, the Tariff Reform I League, the Primrose League, or the Liberal League. (Laughter.) The Government hoped to combine efficiency with economy. They did not want any , remount scandals nor did they want to pay fancy prices for rotten hay. (Laughter). TEIE HOME RULE BILL. Mr. J. HowEs, JUN. (Lancaster),re-opened the debate on the Home Rule Bill. He declared himself to be a Home Ruler, for he was an Englishman and a lover of fair play, and believed in Home Rule for himself equally as much as for Ireland. Mr. Howes then twitted the Opposition with stickling for details and ignoring ' principles, but it was not surprising when tbey recol- Ilected that one of their leaders. Mr. Chamberlain said that a merciful Providence fashioned them hollow in ; order that they might their principles swallow." I (Laughter.) Mr. Howes turned his attention to the hon. member for Newton, whose absence that night he noted, and said it was refreshing to hear him allude to Ireland as a great nation. The Government admitted that ; statement to bo perfectly accurate and therefore could ; not refuse to grant lreland national rights. The 1 depopulation of Ireland had been caused not by the efforts of the Liberal party to establish justice there " but by the operation of the terrible land laws enated by past Tory Governments and which the Liberal party had endeavoured to remove from time to time. If the attacks on life and property &c., in Ireland were an I argument against Home Rule, then the English Parliament should be aboiished, for law breaking in I I England far exceeded any similar operations in Ireland. , If it were not for the offences arising out of the land complications, Ireland would be a pattern to the Empire for the law-abiding conditions of its citizens. Home Rule, as he understood it, would give to the Irish I the liberty of managing their own domestic affairs. The separation of England and Ireland would be a great calamity and the Government proposed Home Rule to I prevent separation. He challenged the Opposition to 1 point to a single instance where Home Rule had been tried and had failed.

Mr. F. G. TAYLOR (Petersfleld) proceeded to oppose IHome Rule on the grounds of Irish disloyalty, animosity between the two races in that country, and the lack of ability on the part of the people to govern I on a fair and level basis. They had in the Irish party ' itself dissensions which did not throw out any possi- bility of hope that if they had entire self-control they I would be any happier than they were at the present time. I The PREMIER (Mr. J. Upson) said the Government ' and the Opposition differed on the question for two reasons, firstly, because of their conception of empire; ; secondly, because of that prejudice which was the child of ignorance. The conception of the Empire on that side of the House and he believed, with the one excep- tion of Ireland, on the other side, was an association ; of free self-governing peoples, mainly speaking the same language, loyal to the same King— (a Member of the Opposition : " No, no I proud of a history which all had helped to make, holding in common an immortal literature, bound together by no legal ties and obligations which must I be enforced at the point of the sword but by ties of mutual interest, of common aspirations, of sympathy and of toleranee. The one absolute essential in such a , conception of empire was the right of self-government Ito every unit composing that association. Mr. Upson • in alluding to ignorance on political questions caused ; roars of laughter by pointing to the popular conception of the Socialist ft a man with a revolver who stood in the way demanding one's property or one's life, and I then remarking "We have the reality in our friend I Councillor Stott, who would part with his last penny ' rather than see a millionaire come to want." The popular conception of an Irishman was a man with a !great mask, bidink behind the hedge with a gun. "As a matter of fact he is our genial friend Councillor Toole." (Laughter.) Ireland cried for freedom, not for golden i fetters. If the Bill were passed, Ireland instead of

being a discontented pariah of Empire would become strong, vigorous, helpful and loyal member of our great Empire. (Cheers). Mr. A. M. CRO WE (Carlow) said the demand of the Irish party was not for separation but for a Parliament representative of the Irish nation with an executive attached to it, sitting in Dublin !to administer purely Irish domestic affairs. _

The Rev. E. 6.--E.C&RLETON (Truro), who made his first appearance in the House, criticised the Bill chiefly on the proposal to give Ireland an independent Parlia- ment and also allow her to send fifty representatives to the English Parliament.

Durin—g Mr. Carleton's speech an amusing incident occurred, one of the Nationalist members inviting him to accept a, sprig of shamrock and throwing it to him across the floor of the House. _

Councillor STOTT saidthe Socialist and Labour party supported the Bill. The town, he added, had recently been placarded with bills stating that the Government were the slaves of the Socialists and the Nationalists. " As far as being slaves to the Socialist party is con- cerned" said Councillor Stott "I must compliment the Liberal party on the selection of their masters. (Laugh- ter.) I cannot compliment the Opposition on the choice of their's, because their political object always seems to be to bolster up or to support and vote in favour of a horde of blackmailers and exploiters. I refer now to the landlords and the brewers." (Cheers.) Mr. JOHN MAGINN (N. Tyrone) said the hon. mem— ber for Newton was much concerned about boycotting &c. in Ireland. "Itis an astounding thing " said Mr. Maginn "what a crime boycotting is in Ireland, com- pared to what it seems to be in England. (Cheers.) For the last week it has been one of the arguments of the Conservative party, advised by some prominent members of Parliament and great speakers—(laughter) to boycott the poor tradesmen of this country who dare to have an opinion of their own and to support the Government in their attempt to amend the ridiculous state of the Licensing Law." (Loud cheers.) Mr. Maginn contended that Home Rule would not mean dismemberment of the empire or injury to any human being. It was jnst liberty to Ireland to manage her own affairs, such as was granted to every town in this country. Mr. Magian referred to the serious competi- tion in the English labour market of Irishmen who had been driven from their homes by mismanagement and hardship, and said a million of British money spent in Ireland would be better protection to England and the Empire than the building of a battleship that might turn turtle the first time it was used. (Laughter and cheers.)

The debate was also taken part in by Mr. 11. W. Guest, Mr. McMermid and Mr. Felix Maginn. Councillor NIELD (Leader of the Opposition) in lengthy criticism of the Bill said they had had many appeals to their prejudices but very few to practical statesmanship. (Laughter.) He alluded to the difficulty of drawing the line between domestic and imperial affairs and said that although he agreed with extended local self-government Ireland and England must, generally speaking, remain one undivided Kingdom. Mr. J. F. AWBERY (secretary for Ireland) maintained that he had no arguments to combat with reference to the Bill. The Government were told that they were slaves to the Nationalists and Socialists. Had they not within the last few days had evidence that the right hon. gentlemen opposite were slaves of a great monopoly ? (Loud cheers). On a vote being taken, the amendment that the Bill be read that day six months was defeated by 74 votes to 49.

MURPH M.P. was welcomed by Councillor Steel (chairman of committees) and delivered an interesting little speech in which he congratulated the Society on the way in which the debate had been conducted and said he gained his own early experience in a debating society. If any of the members were in London at any time be would be pleased to show them round the House.

THE EDUCATION BILL.

The Education Bill which will be introduced nest Thursday by Mr. S. W. Jeffery, Minister for Education, was laid on the table. It was as follows This measure enacts that

(1). All elementary schools receiving Parliamentary grunts, shall be public elementary schools, maintained by the Local Education Authority, and conducted by them for the purpose of giving instruction in secular subjects only. (2). The Local Education Authority"shall provide accommodation without payment of fees for all childrensresident in their area.

(a) Compulsory school age shall be from five years to 14 years of age. (b) No partial exemption from school attend- ance shall be granted to any child under 13 years of age. (3). All teachers shall be appointed by the local Education Authority. (4). No teacher shall be required, as a condition of employment, to subscribe to any religious creed, or to belong or not to belong to any specified religious denomination, or to give any religious instruction. (5). Parliamentary grant at the rate, and as pro- posed by Mr. McKenna's 1908 Education Bill, shall be paid to all public elementary schools until 1911 when an equalized national tax for education shall be levied. (6). Schools held at present under trusts may. be transferred to local Education Authority, but no pay- ment shall be made by the local Education Authority in respect of such transfer. (a) Any teachers in the school at the time of transfer to the local Education Authority shall con- tinue to hold office under the authority by the same tenure and on the same terms and conditions as hefore the transfer. (b) Any teacher who loses employment 1;y. transfer of schools or by failure to transfer shall receive salary for such period (not exceeding two years) as loss of employment continues and shall be entitled to continue payment to the Elementary School Teachers' Superannuation Fund for two years (if not employed in recorded service) and to reckon the time in respect of which contributions are so paid as if it were recorded service. 8. The Education Authority shall give the following undertaking to the trustees of transferred schools : "An undertaking to put the schoolhouse, properly cleaned, warmed and lighted, at the disposal of the trustees or transferors for the purpose of being used by them .or under their authority for the giving of any religious instruction (consistent with the trusts affect- ihg the schoolhouse in the case of a schoolhouse subject to trusts)immediately before or after any meeting of the school and the teachers now employed in such schools,so long as they bold their present posts and are therefore not subject to further creed tests, may be permitted to give special religious teaching out of school hours." 9. All further provision of schools to be made a charge upon the Imperial Exchequer. 10. This Act to be cited as the Elementary Educa- tion Act, 1908, the same to come into operation on the first day of January, 1909.

GARDEN SUBURBS.

Building to be Commenced at Sankey.

The Garden Suburbs are almost an accomplished fact. Land has been secured at Sankey and at Grappenhall (near the locks) and a e learn that the Warrington Tenants Co., Ltd., has decided to proceed almost immediately i,vith the erection of six houses on the Sankey estate.

CROSS COUNTRY RUNNING.

WARRINGTON WESLEYAN HARRIERS V. MANCHESTER G MINAS tum HA RRIERS. These two clubs met at Warrington last Saturday and a close race resulted in a victory for the home team by 2 points. A distance of eight miles was traversed, and the first man to arrive home was C. Vose (the champion of Sankey Harriers, who was assisting the home team, he being their ex-champion), followed by G. Brereton, G. Hampton and W. Lewis, all of Manchester. The teams finished as follows : Warrington Wesleyans, 1, C. Vose ; 5, J. Massey ; 6, H. Gill ; 7, J. Gibson ; 9, W. Thornley ; total 28 points. Manchester Gymnasium, 2, G. Brereton ; 3, G. Hampton ; 4, W. Lewis ; 8, W. Humphreys; 13, ; total 30 points. In conjunction with same, the Wesleyans decided their club championship and novice races, and both caused an amount of keen rivalry. Vose not being qualified for the championship, the winner was J. Massey, who finished a couple of yards only ahead of H. Gill, who was followed by J. Gibson. The novice medals were won by J. Gibson, W. Thornley and W. Drury. A good afternoon's sport was followed by tea and social, Messrs. Hartley and Sharrocks of Manchester, Mr. T. Attwell and Miss L. Evans of Warrington, rendering songs or recitations, Mr. P. Parkes being the. accompanist.

NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN, that the next GENERAL QUARTER SESSIONS of the Peace for the COUNTY PALATINE OF CHESTER will be held at the COURT-HOUSE, KNUTSFORD, in and for the said County, on MONDAY, the 6th day of April next, at Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon, when, the Grand Jury will be empannelled. The hearing of all Appeals arising within the Petty Sessional Divisions of Altrincham, Bucklow,Dukinfleld, Middlewich, Sandbach, Congleton,Northwich, Prestbury and Stockport, forming the Knutsford District, and the trials of all prisoners committed from such Divisions, will take place at such Sessions. The Grand and Petty Juries for the Knutsford District must be in attendance at the Court House, Knutsford, at the opening of the Court on MONDAY, the 6th day of April neat, at Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon. THE COURT will be Adjourned to the Castle of CHESTER on WEDNESDAY, the Bth day of April next, at Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon. The bearing of all appeals arising within the Petty Sessional Divisions of Biro:con, Chester Castle, Daresbury, Eddisbury, Nantwich, Runcorn, Winsford, and Wirral], forming the Chester District, and the trials of all prisoners com- mitted from the last-mentioned Divisions will take place at such Adjourned Sessions. The Grand and Petty Juries for the Chester District must be in attend- ance at, the Castle of Chester, at the opening of the Court on WEDNESDAY, the Bth day of April next, at Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon. REGINALD POTTS, Clerk of the Peace for the County Palatine of Chester. Chester, 18th March, 1908.

The Late Mr. Edmund Whitham. On Sunday evening a memorial service to the late Mr. Edmund Whitham, of 3, Boundary-street,Padgate- lane, a member of the Orford Old Boys' Association and a prominent member of the Manchester Unity of Oddfillows was held at Orford Church. The service - was conducted by the Rev. G. Vale Owen, curate-in- charge of Orford, assisted by Councillor Steel and Mr. John Cann,rnaster of Orford School, and President of the Orford Old Bey' Association. Among others present were P.P.G.M. William Holbrook, P.P.G.M. 1 T. P. Bradshaw, Prov. D.G.M. George F. Freeman, i 4 P.G. T. Reddish,P.G. Welfare, and Mr. G. H. W.Steel. The service was of a very impressive character , thforghont, among the hymns sung being "Jean. Lover of my soul" and "On the resurrection morning. The preacher was the Rev. G. Vale Owen who said they all regretted that their friend had been removed at the early age of 33, but they felt assured he was quite prepared to meet his God. They sympathized very sincerely with the widow and family in their sad Joss, and trusted that God's blessing would rest upon them and comfort them.

Observations made on the Pie du Midi, in the Pyrenees, confirm the results previously obtained at lower altitudes, which shew that both the sun p.nd the moon induce a positive electric charge on the earth. The solar charge varies from one to six volts per minute, according to the state of the atmo- sphere. It is absorbed by clouds and by layers of moist air. The full moon induces a positive electric induction analogous to that caused by the sun.
